the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the healthcare sector is not left behind. the new directive makes care a tradable commodity. the most affluent are first helped where the care is most plentiful and cheapest. the proposal maintains the division of care. i wonder how many of my colleagues can relate to this. care is a basic need that should be equally accessible to all. the members who vote in favour tomorrow may think they are doing a service to the patients, but that is not the case at all. i am in favour of anything that promotes the rights of the patient, but if this directive is adopted, we can expect a lot of trouble in this context; i'll give you a few examples. insurance companies will gladly send patients across the border if there is a cheaper treatment option. this will reduce the availability of care in your own country. care that is no longer profitable will disappear from more expensive regions. patients have no choice, no right, but they have to cross the border. patients from regions where care is cheaper will not have the opportunity to move to another region. the additional costs are not automatically reimbursed. the health tourism envisaged by this directive is therefore reserved for the rich. then the cheap regions and the regions with short waiting times. they will eventually receive so many health tourists that their own local care will be supplanted. we read an example in the belgian newspaper de standaard on monday and i can assure you that more examples will follow. i believe that every patient has the right to good care close to home, in his own language and with his own family. instead, europe is encouraging care abroad and letting the market do its job. whoever agrees with this, vote in favour tomorrow; those who agree with me should certainly vote against.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, religious conviction is an individual matter with a different meaning to different people. the role of the state is to protect all citizens, regardless of their conviction, and to allow them to freely associate and express themselves across the world. a glance at our world today shows a very bleak picture: from the bah’ai in iran to copts in egypt, from the use of blasphemy laws in pakistan to the attacks on christians or places of worship in iraq and nigeria. today we highlight the increased violence against christians specifically, which provides a reason for great concern. it is incredibly painful that people attack and get attacked, both in the name of religion, and people of all faiths are victims. it is unacceptable to see that some individuals, extremists, choose to use violence and even terrorism and do so in the name of god or in the name of a religion, claiming thereby to speak on behalf of others or even seeking to be above the law and thus associating countless innocent people with this violence. but let us not be mistaken. by giving the extremists more credit than they deserve, by accepting the link between religion and terrorism, we disqualify the majority of believers who, in the diverse ways in which they do, practice their faith peacefully. terrorism is political and terrorism is a crime. religion can never be a credible or acceptable reason to use violence or to breach human rights. too often this does happen, not only through violence but also in limiting freedom of expression such as through the abuse of blasphemy laws. religion or conviction should never be a reason for people to live in fear and the european parliament rightly speaks out against the extremist acts against christians and supports those who condemn this extremism.

for decades the european union has pursued a protectionist trade policy, including in the banana sector. import taxes are supposed to deter latin american bananas and at the same time subsidies are given away to poorly competitive exporting countries. the bulk of these subsidies go to the regions outside the european union, almost eur 300 million a year: the european union is a major player in the world economy. the european union is all too happy to present itself as the champion of international law and free trade, always ready to point the moral finger at others. but when it comes to the european union, people prefer to turn a blind eye. everything is permissible, as long as the unprofitable banana industry on islands such as french guiana, the azores and the canary islands is protected. the fact that the european taxpayer is paying for the hundreds of millions of euros in subsidies wasted every year is taken for granted. it is high time that this european mismanagement was abolished and that the european union stopped subsidizing this economic inefficiency.
this parliament is asking what every parliament in the world is asking: democratic control over the formulation and implementation of policy. the european union must be a model, an example, and the treaty of lisbon has been a great step forward for the influence of the european parliament. there is a large majority in this house which does not accept attempts by the council and the commission to reduce parliamentary control in contravention of the spirit of the treaty. this house does not want to micromanage, it simply wants to influence the policy as it is set and implemented. it is therefore a question of the strategic choices, of the content of that policy. in other countries people are taking to the streets to demand that influence, and we must support them in every way possible. but the struggle for democracy is sometimes also a struggle for negotiation and dry notes. the conviction is not changed by it, every power demands maximum democratic control. that is the right of all citizens, in cairo, in tunis and in brussels.

community of values which has left behind a divided past, choosing a united future. we must call on the albanian authorities to do the same and not be suffocated by the shadows of the past, but to requires democratic institutions, the rule of law and its strength is determined by respect for minorities. democracy is not ‘winner takes all’. eu accession is a long journey which requires hard work and shared commitment, and the reforms albania has made are best served by collaboration and continuing commitment to the eu process.
for the greens/efa group the crisis in albania is not new. the crisis did not start in january with the violence against protesters, nor did the crisis start in 2009, when the socialist party boycotted parliament after elections and began the hunger strike. the crisis is a crisis of political culture. a boycott, a hunger strike, demonstrations, these are normal tools in albanian politics. corruption, media influence and political appointments are also. and all this is very far from the policy we would wish for a country on the way to accession. i would like to wish the people of albania a responsible government and a responsible parliament, with politicians who put the welfare of their citizens above their own honour and interests. both sides need to move to get it done. the government has a special responsibility. a government has a monopoly on violence, and a government has an extra responsibility to stand above parties, to do what's right for the people. it is to be expected that this government will do everything in its power to prevent a repeat of what happened in january. that she cooperates fully with an independent inquiry into what happened in january. that it also tackles corruption within its own ranks and that it provides scope for parliamentary debate. the opposition has a responsibility too. it must also do everything possible to prevent a repetition of the violence in january by cooperating fully with the good and fair elections in may, by participating in a parliamentary debate, preferably also once on the substance, such as the high unemployment in albania. but how do you get both sides to go that far? i had all the hope when commissioner füle put the ball in albania's court in november and said: no more steps until you're out. and then the candidate status can be granted. but unfortunately, that didn't change anything. i now hope that mr lajcak will succeed where others have failed to get both parties around the table and make a breakthrough. i do have hope, but not so much faith that i can do this without a stick behind the door. as far as i am concerned, we are considering a sanction. it must be clear that the failure of this attempt at negotiation cannot be without consequences. and sanctions should be directed at political leaders, not at citizens. so we should not be turning our backs on visa-free travel, as i have heard in the corridors. we would really hurt the civilians. i would very much like the commissioner to give me an assurance that visa liberalisation will not be abused in this political process. what we could do, for example, is to think about taking away albania's control of ipa funds and putting it back into eu hands. all the major investments in the country can then continue, but the government is more or less put under the control. that seems to me to be a sanction that does not affect the citizens, but the politicians. and i hope above all that all this is completely unnecessary, because both sides are willing to compromise and mr lajcak will succeed well in the interests of the albanian people.

in writing. − the affordability of pension systems is a problem that has been a hot potato for too long in europe. the percentage of pensioners is rising. in order to keep pensions affordable, member states have no choice but to reform their pension systems. this is directly linked to the sustainability of public finances and the rules for a healthy euro area, the stability and growth pact. i wholeheartedly support the rapporteur, mrs oomen-ruijten, in pointing out the importance of pension accumulation through the employer (the so-called second pillar) and individual pension accumulation (the third pillar). unfortunately, we cannot assume that a state pension (the first pillar) will correspond to the level at which workers have arranged their lives. the subsidiarity aspect needs further elaboration. at the euro area level, agreements are needed to ensure that member states reform their pension systems to ensure sustainable public finances. the choices made in this area should, in my view, remain a matter for the member states themselves. this is the case in social and labour market policy, for example in relation to the participation of women in the labour market. where people voluntarily opt for parttime work in order to better combine work, family and care duties, this should not be discouraged by europe.
in writing. - i voted today in favour of the oomen-ruijten report on pensions. as rapporteur for the imco opinion on this report, i have been able to work constructively with the rapporteur. the imco opinion was largely taken over by mrs oomen-ruijten. i believe that the report is balanced and balanced, with the core principle of subsidiarity for the member states on the subject of pensions, which provides sufficient protection for the dutch pillar system. what i cannot support at all, however, are those parts of the report which give the impression of calling for binding european agreements on the pension systems of the member states. this is contrary to the sp's thinking.
in writing. - the vvd delegation in the european parliament voted today against the own-initiative report on the green paper on european pensions because of several ambiguities and inaccuracies in the report. we support a number of improvements, such as encouraging a good pension mix between state and company pensions in the member states and a european tracking system for more labour mobility, so that every citizen knows what to expect in retirement and can make his own choices. however, rising unemployment, financial market problems, higher national debts and slow economic growth may lead to difficulties in the payment of pension obligations by member states, with implications for the growth and stability pact and thus for the stability of the euro. in addition, the increasing ageing population has a major impact in many member states because savings have not been made as in the netherlands. therefore, there is a need for harmonised supervision of pension systems and some member states should reorganise their pension systems. we do not want the dutch pension system to be affected in any way. to be sure, we voted for some good points in the report but we are against the report as a whole. the vvd-euro group: the commission has also adopted a proposal for a regulation on the
in writing. - europe and pensions, a sensitive subject. several member states are devising mechanisms to reduce their national debt by including pension funds in the debt burden. mrs merkel and mr sarkozy have already mentioned the centralisation of pensions in their plans for a centrally governed europe. i am against the idea of a european pension system. the pension systems in the member states are too different. they're hardly comparable. the pension systems in the member states also vary widely in terms of size, reliability and supervision. i do not want the robust dutch pension system to bleed for the mess that has been made of it elsewhere. what i do think is that it is wise for member states to share information about their pension systems and to make efforts to solve specific bottlenecks. i am referring, for example, to university researchers who move to other member states where they are then taxed by the tax authorities on a pension earned elsewhere. that kind of excrement should be removed immediately. but we do not have to change the whole system to european standards.
in writing. − candidate country croatia wants to conclude its accession negotiations with the european union in june 2011. i am satisfied with the progress of the negotiations, but i want to see action first. i do not want us to regret croatian accession in the future: the accession of bulgaria and romania is a hard lesson. on paper, the fight against corruption is clearly one of the priorities of the zagreb government. but you see, very few corruption cases actually go to trial. the vast majority of cases remain in the investigative stage. this is particularly the case with senior officials. i want to see action from zagreb. the country will receive some eur 4 billion upon accession. the croatian politicians who are going to spend it must be trustworthy. we must learn from the experience of bulgaria and romania. these countries have joined far too quickly and corruption has led to all sorts of wrong pockets being filled with european money. so, as long as the fight against corruption remains at the current level, we cannot complete the negotiation process with croatia, it's as simple as that.

i am describing here a real danger that egypt is facing, namely falling prey to the muslim brotherhood. and i call on us to face that danger, and the commission to face it, and to send an important signal to the egyptians now that if they choose the muslim brotherhood and sharia, they can forget our support. i think that is a very important signal today and i hope you will support me in that.

mr president, i do not think that the independent media creates violence and i do not see the relevance of this question, but i thank the asker of the question for her question.
mr president, tomorrow we will vote on the free trade agreement with south korea. i have to say, it looks good on the surface. the pvv is a strong supporter of free trade. unfortunately, the european union cannot help adding a safeguard clause to the trade agreement. this is to protect non-competitive companies if they cannot compete with korean products. in other words, if the european consumer prefers to buy korean products to european ones because they are sometimes better or cheaper, then trade barriers are being raised again. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. mr president, free trade without any disadvantage to european industry is of course an illusion. the idea of replacing this by a safeguard clause and compensation for the disadvantaged industries is a sham solution. this allows industries with poor competition to continue their activities rather than adapt to competition. worse, who pays the bill here in the end? right, that's the burger, unfortunately. as consumers they pay too much for their purchases and on top of that as taxpayers they have to collect the necessary compensation. how can i explain to my voters that they will be in favour of it if it turns out that fiat, for example, is losing market share to kia? mr president, these arrangements have no place in a free trade agreement. in practice, it means nothing more than subsidizing inefficient companies at the expense of the european citizen. free trade, excellent, but let us please drop this safeguard clause and compensation scheme.
this trade agreement with south korea is ambitious and comprehensive. major barriers to trade will be removed, which will benefit european companies. the increased access to the south korean market will improve the market position of european companies. this is very important, given that other major players such as the united states, chile and the efta countries are also pursuing or have already concluded a trade agreement with south korea. the european union is demonstrating through this agreement that it is not closed off from open and sustainable trade, despite the protectionist tendencies of others. trade is a way out of the economic crisis. but free trade must not lead to free exploitation. the steel and automotive sectors are not worried for nothing. the implementation of the agreement therefore requires close monitoring of compliance with all conditions and the swift and effective use of bilateral safeguard clauses.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - the rapid economic growth in developing countries is passing by large groups of people. even in african countries, economies sometimes grow at double digits, and you can see that in big cities like addis ababa, but inequality is growing just as fast. women and farmers in particular are not given sufficient opportunities to build their own livelihoods. one of the major barriers is access to financial services. private banks simply have more faith in fast-money traders than in women and farmers. here a public bank has an irreplaceable role. the european investment bank is therefore taking an essential step with this legislative resolution. the bank is no longer primarily for dams and roads, for the major infrastructure projects of european companies. this branch of the bank's activities must continue, but of course with procedures in which all local stakeholders are heard, according to clear criteria of sustainability and social justice. tax havens should not have a chance in these projects. the eib is also now opening up its lending to citizens with small businesses in developing countries. this can be done through local banks, through microfinance institutions, although this requires an additional amendment, and through cooperation with the development banks of the member states. the eib will need to be given the space to further develop the necessary expertise in-house with sufficient staff. but with this new dimension of its activities, the eib is an indispensable instrument for the european union's development policy, with a clear commitment to equal opportunities in developing countries, in sustainable economic growth. i thank the eib, i thank mr kalfin and all the rapporteurs for the excellent cooperation.
the eib supports the european union's external policy, particularly its development policy. that is what mr kalfin, a socialist, said in his report. but the pvv does not want any foreign policy from the eu and certainly not a bank in the role of politician. the eib is operating outside the eu, i don't know why, but the eib budget is guaranteed by the eu budget and paid for by the taxpayer. there are some really beautiful countries that are receiving this eib money, such as the palestinian territories, gaza, libya, cuba, venezuela, turkmenistan and the maldives. all of them countries which are not particularly democratic. and now the commission wants to increase the financial ceilings of the eib. we're also going to invest in climate change in these countries, of course. can you see it? in gaza, in cuba? it's material for a new mr. bean movie, but unfortunately it's happening for real! the pvv is firmly opposed to this proposal and will therefore vote against it wholeheartedly. all civil society organisations are faced with a huge lack of resources. this applies to trade unions, consumer organizations and representatives of small and mediumsized enterprises. they all have to do it from a small office in brussels, unlike large companies that have the means to hire professional lobbyists. this means that, in the interests of workers, consumers and smes, it is important that people from outside brussels should be able to take part in expert groups. but there is a price. the commission's website should clearly indicate that travel and subsistence expenses are reimbursed, and that there is also a reimbursement scheme for other costs directly related to the work of expert groups. ngos are often unaware of this and not all commission officials voluntarily bring it to their attention. for example, it is impossible for a small selfemployed person to leave his or her business for a moment without compensation to take part in a group of experts in brussels. such reimbursement is therefore essential. dg sanco considers that experts appointed in a personal capacity should not have links with organisations that have a direct interest in the subject of the expert group. i would ask the commissioner to make the policy of dg sanco mandatory for all dgs. finally, i would like to hear from the commissioner how it is that the commission's website mentions greenpeace as a participant in the implementing geological disposal of waste - technology platform, when i have in my possession the correspondence between greenpeace and the commission which shows that greenpeace's application for participation has been explicitly rejected by the commission.

the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. it invites us to celebrate what we have achieved, such as women's suffrage, equal treatment and reproductive rights, at least in the more civilized countries. this day also invites mourning for what is still wrong. in the eu, women are still severely underrepresented in decisionmaking, care is almost exclusively for women, and there is still a persistent income gap between men and women, even outside the eu where some countries have not even started to give women the right to vote. the 100th women's day also invites a historical comparison. the first really big step for women in the labour market was taken during the world wars, especially the second. there were simply not enough men to do the work in factories and offices. employers had no choice, and women entered the labour market in large numbers. we're in that period again. this time, fortunately, not with war, but with aging. in five to ten years there will simply be too few men to fill vacancies at all levels. employers must attract and retain women. ageing is a great opportunity for gender equality. and it is up to us as european politicians and our colleagues in the national parliaments to encourage this development with good maternity leave for mothers and fathers, with adequate affordable childcare, with the right to flexible hours and parttime work with good social rights, and with quotas for business closures and politics or the credible threat thereof. together we can ensure that it will not take another hundred years before men and women have equal rights and equal opportunities in all areas.
in writing. - i cannot support the podimata report. the member states are responsible for tax policy. the report does not make this situation right, but seeks to impose a number of taxes from the eu, including a financial transaction tax and an energy tax. if a tax on the financial sector is necessary, the proceeds should go to the treasury of the member states. after all, they had to bail out the banks. member states are wise to use this revenue to reduce the public debt incurred during the crisis. the report, on the other hand, explicitly assumes that the proceeds can benefit global and eu policy objectives. that would be climate change and development cooperation. the report also calls for a solidarity levy on airline tickets to combat public health problems and charges for the transport sector. i understand that there are useful policy objectives to be pursued and that more revenue is considered desirable for this purpose. however, since it concerns tax measures, this must be initiated from the member states and not dictated top-down from europe. i am afraid that such a move would be an obstacle to fruitful cooperation with the council.

president. - i should like to thank mr bernd lange for his excellent report and for the excellent cooperation. the industrial policy has returned from the past, and not only back but also not alien. together with two other flagships, two other strategies, this strategy is at the heart of the european agenda for jobs and a healthy economy. and most importantly, not just now, but in the future, a viable future. industrial policy must be integrated with all other strategies on the innovation union, raw materials and the efficient use of natural resources. these three form the core. it should be a holistic approach, with intensive cooperation between different policy areas. are they really two different policy areas? and where industry - as you, commissioner, have already said - is not the enemy but the supporter of sustainability. industrial policy cannot be pursued without an integrated market. the internal market is still in the making and needs to be completed. the whole industry, but especially small innovative companies, will benefit from this. producers can be creative, but they must also be responsible, and that means ultimate product responsibility. the aim is to encourage cleaner production and more sustainable products, because i believe that is ultimately the future of europe. and that's what we're going to do with this cradle-to-cradle industry. and with that, we create a smart resource management and a healthy economy. industrial policy has been neglected for too long and left to the market. it's time we set an example for others and for ourselves again.
the manufacturing industry, mr president, commissioner, will remain important, also in the knowledge economy of today and tomorrow. it remains essential to maintain high quality production in europe and at the same time it is the cork on which the knowledge and skills of many in our countries float and our competitiveness is developed. this report rightly places industry high on the european agenda and this is important for large-scale industry as well as for small and mediumsized enterprises. we in europe have the best knowledge in many fields. i am thinking of the energy sector, the aerospace industry or the automotive industry, which is turning like a whistle at the moment. i am thinking of the supply of the automobile industry, which is essential for europe. and how clever and clean the eu 2020 strategy is! but i have one more point: the second is globalisation. we have a globalisation adjustment fund and we will soon have the european patent. nevertheless, commissioner tajani has put equal competition in the world on the agenda. it is all right for chinese companies to invest in europe, but we must have the same opportunities there. and if it is true that large amounts of public money are being invested here, then something is wrong. it is not a matter of returning to protectionism - mr audy has just said this - but of the necessary transparency in essential sectors such as ict and other important european sectors. no european veto, that is the shareholders, that is the supervisors, but it is very important to look at this as europe. it is not wrong to start a debate on this subject in the light of mr lange's excellent report. it's being prosecuted. commissioner tajani, i would like to invite you to do so.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we can go and get the raw materials, but we would rather keep them. maybe we can even start making them. i welcome your initiative for a new partnership, including a replacement, but i do think that other colleagues have said this and are right: we have to try to keep them, keep them in europe. that's very important. we need to increase the collection. we need to shift to other business models to simply get as much of the rare earth metals back for recycling as possible. we can do that through innovation, and that requires more efficiency. but what i think is very important: are you also going to pay attention to the fact that these rare earth metals are going to the most useful use when they become even more rare? do you notice that at even higher rarity, eventually those applications end up being left behind that we all need the most, that are the most urgent, for example, that it doesn't end up in a toy from some mcdonalds, but rather in a windmill? will you pay attention to that? because that seems very, very important to me.
the european parliament has been asked to take a decision on the situation in libya, and i am sure that the commission will be able to do so. meanwhile, murder is the order of the day in the capital tripoli, and countless civilians are disappearing without a trace. a tragic situation that underscores the opinion of an experienced libyan traveler: the only effective means is probably armed intervention. i can, however, understand the western reluctance to accept this last resort. i can, however, offer considerably less sympathy for the delay in imposing a no-fly zone over libyan territory. in the end, inactivity in these areas comes at a high price: the european union is not prepared to accept the death penalty. the european union can and must help lay the foundations for a libya after gaddafi, for a better future for this tribal state. make brother commander, as gaddafi calls himself, literally a wing lamb first.

vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. − mr president, i think that this has been an interesting debate and i wanted to just deal with the two specific issues that were raised in a sense outside of the debate before winding up on that matter. ms paulsen asked me whether we could find space in the – i think the word was ‘glittering’ – new building, to be able to have people working on the issues of concern in human rights. can i just say that i was delighted that madam hautala, the chair of the subcommittee on human rights, wrote to me to say that she was pleased with the design of how we plan to deal with human rights within the new organisation. however, i am only moving people into one building to remove them from eight buildings where they are currently scattered around brussels, believing as i do that enabling people to work together will make us more effective. we will have to squeeze them in, because i am also trying to save money on the way. therefore i hope, when you see what we do, that the word ‘glittering’ will be a nice word and not a word, i fear, that suggests i am trying to do something that i genuinely am not. in response to ms sinclaire: the way that i was told to respond on human rights issues by parliament, by decision of this house, was to attend regularly the committee on foreign affairs (afet) to which the subcommittees – of which the subcommittee on human rights is one – would be invited and where they would be able to put questions to me. i have done that and i am more than happy to meet with ms hautala, as i do, and to respond to queries from her, as well as to attend the plenary debates in this house. i am more than happy to try and do more; i simply ask you to recognise that there is a limit to the amount of hours i have got in the day and the number of things i can do. but it is not out of any disrespect to the committee or indeed to you personally at all; far from it. i do hope that you will feel able to contact my office with issues of concern and indeed to attend the meetings where i am able to come and speak. i am very well aware of my own background in terms of being a leader of a house of parliament and not being elected, but i was very privileged to be elected by this house and of course to be invited into this role by 27 heads of state and the president of the commission. as far as that goes i am very mindful of my responsibilities. in relation to the debate itself and the human rights issues that have in a sense dominated our debate: i am trying to look at what more we can do. i will not comment on what currently is being dealt with specifically because i want to present this when we finish the work, but we are talking to human rights activists and to organisations, as well as considering across the 27 member states what might be a more effective way of being able to address this issue and to put as much pressure as we possibly can. as regards mr karoubi and mr mousavi, as i have said, it is even difficult to establish precisely whether they are in prison or under house arrest. i am trying to continue to get that clarification. of course we are supporting the resolution in geneva for a special rapporteur on iran. we do raise these issues directly as well; it is really important that we all stand together in pushing forward on human rights in iran. many honourable members have raised specific cases and the terrible record that this country has, and we need to do as much as we possibly can and i think we can do better if we do that together. finally, on the nuclear issue: i spent four and half days in direct discussions with the iranian negotiators and i am very clear in what i am trying to do, which is to provide an opportunity for this country, for iran, to show what it keeps saying, to demonstrate that it is moving in a civil nuclear power direction and not in a nuclear weapons direction. as i have said to them, it is not difficult: what they have to do is be open and transparent, to allow the inspectors to do the job they are supposed to do and to do the confidence building that we have ofttimes explained to them in great detail. it is for them to choose. i am absolutely clear as well that the security council and my role with the e3+3 will continue to put pressure on to try and make sure this happens. what is really encouraging is that although i lead the negotiations, i do so with the e3+3 sitting with me; and that shows very clearly to iran that we are totally united in our approach and i pay tribute to all those who work with me. they have to show the political will to do what is necessary; and if they do not, i say to the honourable members, we will have to do more to keep the pressure on.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, first of all, ladies and gentlemen, may i thank you very much for your valuable contributions to this debate. i listened to that very carefully. your contribution makes clear what i intend to do with this report, namely to indicate that our fundamental rights, our human rights which are so fundamental and form the basis of our existence, are also the rights of the iranians. that's identical for me. in other words, the attention we give to fundamental rights and their observance is essential. i am convinced that this will also create a future for iran, and in particular for the young people of iran. i was struck during the debate by what mrs lösing - she is unfortunately no longer here - said about the future. i'm sure that means a lot to me. let's look at the facts. for years now, more than 150,000 young, highly educated iranians have been leaving the islamic republic precisely because of the lack of prospects. not only because of the repression, but also because of the oppressive socioeconomic situation and the climate of unfreedom. that amounts - and an insider, an economist, told me that - to a loss of 40 billion a year alone, not counting the brain drain. i would like to point out in my report that our attention and our encouragement to open up more space and respect for human rights there really do give a future perspective. this will also benefit the region in terms of overall security. finally, madam high representative, i was very curious - on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the un human rights council has the potential to address the ever more urgent and elaborate human rights violations across the world in different countries – iran, belarus, burma, tunisia, libya, etc., there are too many to mention – but also horizontal themes such as lgbt rights, free expression, rape as a war crime and the role of human rights defenders. in a sense, the un human rights council is in a similar position to the eu and it has to act as a global player. but both bodies suffer from the same problem – a lack of credibility, double standards at times, and they are not able to act quickly enough. the eu thus far has been unwilling to scrutinise its own human rights record sufficiently, for example its participation in renditions and the lack of press freedom in some of our member states. until last week libya was a member of the un human rights council. it has, rightfully, been removed. this is a very important precedent.
i would like to draw your attention to two points in your introduction. you spoke of direct negotiations. last week i had the privilege of speaking at length with the representatives of the israeli government and also of the israeli parliament, the knesset. you have to trust and believe the people you're talking to. they say they are committed to direct negotiations with the palestinian authority. i would therefore ask you to use all your diplomatic ingenuity to bring about these direct negotiations, because in the end, peace requires that both sides in the conflict be directly at the table. and in the light of the events in which arab masses are calling for accountable governments, we can conclude that now is the momentum to make a breakthrough. the second point: it was very good of you to refer to the sad fate of gilad shalit, who has been imprisoned for almost five years now. i spoke to his father exactly a week ago in the heart of jerusalem, and he asked whether europe still cared about his son. you have given me the answer. i hope you will continue to work hard to reunite father and son as soon as possible.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, i understand the urgency of the commission in addressing the economic crisis and the high levels of debt which are a threat for the stability of the euro. i understand that conditions are imposed on countries that have to make use of the european financial stability mechanism. however, it seems to me that the commission is being quite selective in the measures and conditions it deems permitted in this crisis. on the one hand, nearly any measure seems legal when it comes to fiscal consolidation by cutting expenditure. as soon as ireland made use of the rescue package, minimum wages and pension levels were the first variables to be adjusted, despite the fact that these matters explicitly are not eu competences. on the other hand, the commission hides itself behind the lack of eu competence to leave the exceptionally low irish corporation tax untouched. whereas increasing this could also substantially improve public revenues in ireland. cutting expenditure is not, after all, the only way to achieve a balanced budget. i understand that this crisis requires exceptional measures, but why is the commission in such a hurry to adjust minimum wages, affecting workers who earn the least, while we have to wait and see about measures that make the banking and financial sector pay? i strongly get the feeling that the commission is interpreting the eu’s competences in a very one-sided and, dare i say it, right-wing manner. this blunt approach inspires euro-scepticism. it seems that the eu acts firmly in the field of employment and social policy only in times of crisis, and then does so by imposing cuts in social spending and wages. the eu can only regain confidence if the commission shows the same determination in setting proper minimum social standards and social guarantees for the heavy cuts imposed on people. yes, we do want economic governance, but we want balanced governance with both fiscal sustainability and social guarantees. if not, the vulnerable will pay the price for a mess that they are not responsible for.
mr president, very few people in the netherlands suspect that there are discussions here in brussels about their wages and pensions. we are seeing what is happening in ireland, but in the meantime i am also hearing all kinds of proposals for the european council on the abolition of wage indexation, on maximising wage increases with increases in labour productivity, and so on. unfortunately, there is a deafening silence on the subject of agreements on european minimum wage standards. nor do i hear any proposals to guarantee that workers get paid work and that they get full employment instead of piecework or on-call contracts. i am concerned about the impact of the current discussions on ordinary people, but i am also concerned about the image of the european union. it seems as if the interests of the financial institutions, the speculators and the big companies are the only ones being taken into account. commissioner, will the commission correct that image and ensure that it is seen as an institution which also represents the interests of ordinary people?

president. - in the committee on foreign affairs, the high representative made a specific statement yesterday afternoon on his intention to support the so-called 'new egypt' in combating the widespread housing shortage. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is a question of genuine european empathy. i therefore warmly welcome all european efforts to improve the living conditions in egypt. especially as the country is in a very uncertain transition phase after the departure of president mubarak, and cairo seems to be heading straight for a financial and food crisis. i believe that european aid to egypt must be accompanied by a twofold commitment on the part of the council. in the new egypt, all citizens, regardless of their religion, should be equal before the law, with equal rights for all. the european union is also committed to the peace treaty with israel, and to developing it further. the setting of these two conditions strengthens both the internal and external stability of the new egypt.

and i hope it does not - is that we as the european union are drifting further and further away from the citizens of europe. i think we really need to start focusing and investing in the coming years on how we can get individual citizens to support the european union and what we do. i think there is a lot to be done, and we need to invest in it and we need to ask the communications managers how best to do that. secondly, i see that the public does not know the difference between the european commission, parliament and the council. in each member state we have three representatives who are not allowed to do anything because they work for official organizations. if there is negativity about the european union in the member states, there is no one who can stop it. i think it would be useful to consider having one representative on behalf of the european union in the member states - call it an ambassador - who could eventually respond to any negative talk about the european union. if we do that, we'll prevent ourselves from becoming the titanic, because on the titanic, everyone thought everything was great, but in the end, danger was coming from outside.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs. - i thank the commission and the council for the opening of this morning's debate. i have written the opinion on the internal market with great interest from the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. i think that the single market should be supported by the european parliament in a broad sense, but especially by the committee on civil liberties, because the free movement of persons is particularly important within the union, of labour migrants and skilled migrants, who we need to increase the labour market in that single market. but, mr president, there are many intentions, but not enough results. i am very pleased with the 12 levers of commissioner barnier and i hope that we can speed things up in the coming months, because competition outside the eu does not wait for the internal market within the eu.
mr president, let me start with the positive. the reports before us mention a number of good elements, such as the smooth implementation of the services directive, the european patent and the tackling of excessive roaming charges. these are issues that touch the heart of the single market and help citizens and businesses. unfortunately, i also have to say that many other initiatives completely miss the objective of the single market. i'll name a few: climate and co2 targets, socioeconomic equality, fair labor conditions and of course more solidarity, or socioeconomic redistribution, and certainly more money to spend on it. these are all proposals of a socialist nature which, in our view, do not belong in the european agenda and certainly do not fit in with the internal market. it is a utopia to think that the differences in europe can be brushed away by socioeconomic interventions. the internal market is not designed to create an all-embracing equality in europe. president, the freedom party would prefer a single market that focuses on the core: promoting freedom and economic cooperation. address the backlog of maintenance on this and remove redundant initiatives. the vision of the internal market that is being promoted here is a bridge too far for the pvv.
in discussing the excellent report by mr louis grech, i had the impression that both parliament and the commission were aware of the need to make the internal market more social. we all agreed that the cause of the partial failure of the internal market is that it has won the hearts of big companies but not those of ordinary people. proposals for a social clause to protect trade union rights and collective bargaining agreements have so far failed to pass. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. even the position of the trade union movement is in question. commissioner barnier, do not let yourself be misled. follow your social heart and make sure that the single market is for everyone and not a toy for big companies. that's why i'm calling for: the commission has already made a proposal which makes it clear that social rights must not be subordinated to the principle of unrestricted competition. i have a word with mr sinclaire. it was a pleasure to submit the written statement on smes together with malcolm harbour. we have not had your support and i think your criticism of that is totally unjustified.
mr president, i definitely do not want to reopen the debate, but we really should think twice about giving the floor to people who can put their personal opinion forward under the disguise of scientific evidence. let us just vote. we have made up our minds. (applause)
in writing. the pvv votes in favour of this resolution because there is a clear doubt about the health claim of dha. we do want to see a review of the procedure for granting health claims. the pvv is a strong supporter of independent scientific research and that is why we want a transparent procedure with no doubtful outcome.
on behalf of my group, i would like to thank commissioner oettinger for his very active involvement in the nuclear safety issue immediately after the disaster in japan. this is of great importance for the safety of our citizens, because the nuclear disaster in japan makes it clear that it is necessary to review our nuclear safety rules. this is an important task for you, together with the council, because we in europe are interdependent when it comes to nuclear safety. we need a european stress test for all nuclear installations and the criteria must be harmonised, as you said. that stress test should be reported in an objective and transparent manner, i.e. publicly, taking as a starting point the highest safety level. i hope, mr president, that you will work hard with the member states to achieve that harmonized approach so that we can be sure that the highest safety standards are being applied. and if a nuclear installation does not pass the stress test well, you will ensure, together with the council, that there is a commitment that that member state will take immediate action. it could also be the temporary shutdown or even the permanent closure of a nuclear power plant. it is important to include nuclear power stations across our borders, because they are sometimes even more important to our citizens than nuclear power stations in europe. so take good care of it. we must act with prudence and consider the risks. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not distorted by the fact that the community's energy policy is not in line with the community's energy policy. but we must rethink our future and develop renewable energy sources and energy efficiency in an ambitious way.
mr president, i would like to ask my colleague how many nuclear plants have to go down before she says that it is enough. she talks about only one nuclear plant in japan having been hit. however, there were six nuclear plants, not one. how many would be enough for her to change her opinion?
for the epp group a radical regime change in the sense of a seizure of power by radical islamic forces in syria, bahrain and yemen would amount to a downright disaster scenario both domestically and abroad. the laughing stock in this desperate situation, at least in the arabian peninsula, would certainly be the iranian leadership duo khamenei-ahmadinejad, which would temporarily lose the already small chance of a persian spring. en passant, the crisis situation in damascus, manamah and sanaa offers the eu a prime opportunity to set clear conditions for backing the beleaguered regimes in close coordination with the united states: the commission has also been active in the field of education and training. a few years ago, mr president, we visited syria with a delegation from the european parliament. this is the only secular state in the region that has been hosting hundreds of thousands of fleeing iraqi christians since 2003, a warning sign. the west must do its utmost to ensure that the bloody sectarian civil war in iraq does not continue in syria.
mr president, we are talking today about three very different countries with very specific local conditions. in yemen, the uprising is massive, despite brutal violence by the police and the army. in bahrain there is a great risk of destabilisation of the whole region through the involvement of saudi arabia and iran. finally, in syria, the opposition has practically no space and the regime seems ready for anything. on the basis of these specific circumstances, i believe that the eu should organise bilateral relations with these countries. there are a few principles. there is, of course, a right to demonstrate and to express oneself freely for every citizen. but what do we do about the peaceful protests, where those civilians were at the same time victims of the intervention of the police and the army? do we have to have an international investigation? what is the eu doing to promote this research? secondly, governments have a duty to enter into dialogue with the opposition and civil society organisations, because violence and repression are never the solution. what is the eu doing in practice to promote this dialogue? then, if the regimes in syria and bahrain, but also in yemen, are unable or unwilling to listen to their people, where does that lead when we talk about relations with those countries? we must provide concrete help - as has been said many times here - but that concrete help must also be given a chance. how are we gonna do that? i would have liked to have heard a plan for that today. i would also like to make one further comment, also to the commissioner. i think that in the specific case of syria we should also call on turkey to exert pressure, also in connection with the plans which are apparently to be adopted in the coming days. president. - the next item is the report (doc. i think that our own delegation for relations with the mashreq countries could do a very good job here and that we should do everything we can to enable it to do so.
the european parliament has called for the eu to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu's external action is coordinated with the eu's external action. in the least developed countries in africa, a lot of progress has been made in the last 10 years. these countries worked towards better governance and mobilised their own financial resources. rich countries provided debt relief and vaccine availability, among other things. but we are far from there, because the rapid economic growth in the poorest countries does not bring about a proportional reduction in poverty. the list of the poorest countries has remained virtually unchanged for decades. the number of people living on less than a dollar a day has gone down, but the number of people living on less than two dollars a day has stayed the same. the rich countries preferred to maintain corrupt regimes rather than think about fair shares. it is absurd and cynical to then cry that development policy is not working. we haven't given it enough of a chance. the un conference in istanbul will have to draw the conclusions from this. much more attention needs to be paid to the fair distribution of wealth within the poorest countries themselves. that's what gives us stability, that fair distribution. it relieves tension and brings peace. social justice also brings much more economic development than the inequality of dictatorships with a small clan at the top. countries with democracies achieve that fair distribution much faster than dictatorships. good governance is bringing the mdgs closer. more is also needed for public health. for a family in a poor country, illness is a financial disaster. sickness is a luxury no one can afford. we must therefore work on financing health care with health insurance, so that not only vaccines are available, but also good hospitals and clinics.
can you agree with me that the foreign interests of the rich western countries in europe and the united states have kept dictators in power and that this has often made development aid ineffective because it went into the wrong pockets and we have never done anything about it in the name of stability that did not deserve its name, as we see in north africa and the middle east? standing up for democracy means standing up for democrats in developing countries and toppling dictators, but allowing development aid to continue.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, the committee on foreign affairs has delivered a good and balanced report on the progress of macedonia, and we as greens will gladly support it. on the one hand it is critical of the authorities in macedonia, and more so than last year. they need to work on political stability, they need to stop any provocation of ethnic non-majorities and neighbours, and they need to guarantee freedom of the media and expression. on the other hand, it unambiguously calls for the opening of negotiations. the country complies with the criteria and deserves that. i wish greece would respect the advice of the commission and parliament and stop blocking the accession process. it can always put the brakes on the final decision if it really wants to. i very much hope macedonia will keep working on becoming a mature european democracy, regardless of what greece does. the accession dialogue that commissioner füle just mentioned sounds promising in that respect. reform is a good thing in any case.
on behalf of the s&d group, the end of the power struggle in côte d'ivoire is in sight after more than 1,500 deaths, almost a million refugees in the region, entire streets full of looted houses and an economy that came to a standstill. the country is going through a catastrophe and that is the price laurent gbagbo has paid for refusing to admit defeat. here, a sharp condemnation of his reckless refusal to respect the electorate is in order. it is also appropriate to condemn all the violence of the past few months, the many violations of human rights, the threats, the violence against un staff, the incitement to hatred and the kidnappings. there's still shooting, but there's negotiations about gbagbo's departure, and that's good news. but in ivory coast, there is an emergency. many ivorians now lack food and water because they did not dare leave their homes. it is essential that president alassane ouattara be given full support in returning to the rule of law, where people can live in peace and without fear, and where freedom of the press is restored. that's the first task now. the president has a duty to prevent his troops from using force against the population and it is good that ouattara has launched an inquiry into the conduct of the shocking massacre in duékoué. whether the ivorian people restore the rule of law through the courts or through a truth and reconciliation commission, one thing is clear: war crimes do not run out of time. the international criminal court must be allowed to do its job. the sanctions have had an impact, gbagbo was financially drained. but now we must ease the sanctions quickly, once ouattara has taken his rightful seat. because everything is at a standstill, even programmes to provide medicines to people with hiv-aids in côte d'ivoire are now in jeopardy. president. - the donors of côte d'ivoire must not abandon the country now.

in the run-up to this debate, i have re-read the open letter from svetlana alexiyevich, the most important intellectual voice in belarus, to president alexander lukashenko. she writes: our people are afraid of the revolution too. but as in the past, no one wants to live. before the elections, the political dialogue in our society had just begun and you immediately and grossly broke it. once again, the country is paralyzed by fear. end of quote. alexeyevich calls on lukashenko to resume the dialogue with the people. is that a naive thought? after all, in an interview published on january 31, 2011 in the neue züricher zeitung, the writer speaks of lukashenko as the hostage-taker of the entire belarusian nation, the dictator of the little man, over whom only the kremlin can really exert influence. the point is, however, that there is no indication that lukashenko is willing to turn himself in to moscow. the european institutions then offer a strategic opening, despite the current problematic situation. i wish commissioner füle much wisdom, perseverance and certainly the full support of our parliament in finding and exploiting responsible contacts with belarus.

- a writer. - mr president, the situation in the fisheries sector is very serious. fuel prices are high, fish prices are low, historically low. i would like to ask you, colleagues, to pay attention to the fishing in the shoals. the fishing in schools is important for my country, the netherlands, but also for france, belgium, england and denmark. a few years ago, there was a shortage of schools. for this reason, duty-free imports of other flatfish were allowed. cheap alternatives, less tasty ones, but that's beside the point. today, however, there is enough schooling. however, duty-free imports of cheap flatfish will continue. the result: the fresh shoal is sold well below cost or is passed over at auction. i wish! the duty-free import of flatfish fillets must be stopped, mr president. it is not only the fishermen who want this, but also the processors and the trade. - why? - why? the fishermen's survival is essential for the trader, otherwise the trade will not have any stock in its range in the future. colleagues, concrete measures are urgently needed. the crisis calls for european action. i call on you to vote in favour of this measure.
president. - mrs kroes, today is europe day. europe is eager to find concrete issues that matter to its citizens and that also bring prosperity and well-being on a european scale. this subject, this report by mr hökmark, fits that perfectly. it is concrete and brings us closer to both the social and the economic. it's freeing up scarce broadcast space that's badly needed to meet that demand for more and more and faster wireless internet connections. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. in 2013 800 mhz will be released, in 2015 1200 mhz and that is badly needed if europe wants to remain one of the world's leaders. these services have a high economic value: 200 million a year, about 3 to 4% of economic growth depends on sufficient spectrum, and that percentage is only increasing. for example, look at the cellular standard that we created together in the 1990s. that was one of those impulses at the right time. i have to tell you, these economies of scale are ticking. it could make a difference of 80 billion euros a year. the netherlands, my country, which i am also honoured to represent here, is ready. we've already made room for the fourth generation of mobile internet. in short, the problems of the emergency services, the television stations, the public broadcasting and the remaining problems of possible interference, the problems with local microphones, will be solved. i therefore hope that on the basis of this excellent and promising report, an agreement will be reached with the commission at first reading. good luck with that!
mr president, i think there are two views on the free trade agreement with india. first, the view that says: we now have an agreement which is the most extensive in the world, so it must go on, it is important, and then the series of so-called non-trade aspects, such as minority rights, child labour and caste discrimination, will naturally be addressed during the trade flows. i call this the merchant's vision. that view dominates the resolution before us. the second view, mr president, is that we must first of all be clear about the non-trade aspects and then we must conclude an agreement. that's what i call the reverend's vision. we know that in that case india will certainly not make a deal. mr president, i would like to remind this house that in march 2009 we adopted a resolution which looked at both the trade and non-trade aspects. the resolution before us is a step backwards in my view. it has a sustainable development clause, but it's toothless. the dalits are hardly mentioned. nature and the environment are dealt with in the text before us. i therefore do not support the text of the resolution. our negotiators must stick to the 2009 resolution. the merchant and the vicar must continue to work together, and i quote the commissioner, only then will you have a balanced agreement.
madam president, the netherlands, my member state, is a centuries-old trading nation and the commissioner will have to acknowledge this, because we have even formed one country together. dutch companies are among the largest investors worldwide. the netherlands has therefore concluded 98 bilateral investment treaties with a very high level of protection. the proposed authorization system gives the commission too many powers to withhold its approval of existing investment agreements and too little certainty for existing agreements. the imminent possibility that member states may have to terminate their existing bilateral investment treaties at the instigation of the commission before a new agreement with an equivalent level of protection is concluded by the eu is unacceptable. the withdrawal of authorisation may only take place if the eu has negotiated a new agreement with the same level of protection.

madam president, the rapporteur, mr chatzimarkakis, and many other speakers have rightly stressed the need for statements by the member states. it is a bit like asking the council and the member states for a favour. i think that's a misunderstanding. that is why i welcome the council's presence here this morning. perhaps it would be good for the council to read over again what exactly is said in the interinstitutional agreement concluded when we approved the financial perspective for this period. it was then clearly stated that the council would make these declarations at the most appropriate political level. it is a pity that the council does not respect the agreements. but there was something to the contrary, and it is perhaps worth recalling this now that we are negotiating new financial perspectives. there was a difference of opinion between the council and parliament on the level of the financial perspective. the difference was about 112 billion over a seven-year period. parliament went back to 110 billion. in return, we wanted some things that didn't cost money. membership declarations and political accountability do not cost money. the council signed off on that. but what do we find four years later? four member states are complying with the agreement and the other member states are not. that does not bode well for the forthcoming negotiations. we must be in favour of the signature, even if austria was represented in the council at the time. so i think it is essential that the action programme now agreed is actually implemented, because otherwise it is also bad for the negotiations on the new financial perspective. finally, a word on a general matter. i thought this was the seventeenth time that the european court of auditors has not issued a statement of assurance. it will be difficult to maintain the discharge from parliament year after year in the light of the forthcoming european elections. there's got to be a positive das sometime.

in writing. the pvv votes against this recommendation of the european parliament. the original commission proposal sought to simplify formalities in order to facilitate the introduction of new textile fibres. the european parliament is also proposing that companies be required in future to indicate the country of origin of a textile product, the so-called 'country of origin' label. we do not support this position of parliament. consumers benefit from information on labels on materials that pose a danger to public health or that cause allergic reactions. the mention of the country of origin on the label is a useless european gesture. the origin of a product says almost nothing about the production process or the quality of the product, and it does not contribute to consumer protection. even when the so-called country of origin is mentioned, parts of the production process may still take place elsewhere. the 'made in label' only creates a false sense of clarity for the consumer, while it does bring additional costs and red tape. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. the pvv will therefore vote against this recommendation.

- i'm the reporter. - president, commissioner, you are an innovator, i am an innovator, we are innovators, we can all be innovators. how can we be? as a researcher, as a scientist, as a policymaker, as an employee in the workplace, as a consumer, as a citizen. the citizen is at the heart of innovation. he makes or breaks innovation. but what can we do as policymakers, as politicians, to accelerate that innovation? innovation is the way out of the financial and economic crisis, because innovation is going to solve the problems we all face, such as ageing, climate change, the scarcity of raw materials, and it will also put an end to the financial and economic crisis right away. we hope that innovation will bring all of these together, give the economy a big boost, and in the meantime move towards that sustainable society. but that's gonna take some doing. it is a question of a change of mindset, a change of ideas about society and how we should operate in politics and business. what concrete measures could we take? more focus and less fragmentation. we need to get rid of this compartmentalized spirit of innovation and policy. what are we trying to achieve? innovation is not just a matter of one box, one department, one programme, innovation has to be something that runs right through the whole policy. so that must be the mindset of politics. preferably a holistic approach. why do we have all these programs? throw them together where you can and start simplifying things for once. that is our job as politicians. cut the red tape. we as politicians always tend, when we want to achieve something, to add a layer on top, an extra regulation, an extra programme, an extra funding option. perhaps the solution here is not "extra", but rather "less" or "instead of". so less control in the funding applications and what is very important: let us provide services to those who come to us for funding, for incentives, a one-stop shop, a one-stop shop for applicants, for start-ups, for smes, possibly also for regions and research institutions. the evaluation will therefore not only look at the funding request but also, if appropriate, at appropriate partners or networks for cooperation. we need much more risk capital and we really need to raise 5 billion in the next financial period instead of 1 billion, because that is what gives the banks a boost in lending to smes. we need a cheap and simple patent, workable rules to protect intellectual property. a genuine european single market where innovation can thrive by gaining access to a market of 500 million citizens. it's way too compartmentalized right now. until now, innovation has been seen mainly as a high-tech product. it's not that, it could be social innovation. europe has for years been struggling with an outflow of innovation refugees, creative people who prefer to go to the united states, because they think it's easier there, and frankly, sometimes it is. tight personal liability rules, lack of financial support and high barriers to entry into the european market are all barriers that have prevented innovation from being a great success in europe to date. the eu must have the courage to reward, not punish, courage, enterprise, curiosity and creativity. we need european steve jobs; let's find out what we're good at, let's bring together in europe that spirit of inquiry, einstein, and steve jobs.
