rapporteur. − madam president, i would like to thank colleagues and innovative citizens for all their very valuable contributions. it was a very inclusive process. we are in unison here: we want to boost economic growth and move towards a different, sustainable social model. we want to stimulate smes and start-ups; we want to update our skills in lifelong learning; we want social innovation. we want the money actually to be spent on research and development, not to be kept in the pocket. we agree that research is not a synonym for innovation; innovation stands on its own, though it might derive from research. both are extremely important, but innovation needs to get an extra push. to clear up the issue of funding coming from the agricultural funds or the structural funds: the intention is not that we move funding from the cap or the structural funds to the innovation funds, but that we pay attention to the horizontal aspect of innovation. we need innovation in agriculture and in regional policy too: we need it everywhere. so where do we go from here? let us not end with this one report. we cannot let it end here. let us take this onwards, together with the member states. let us really try to get a different mindset. let us spend the money, but let us change the policy. let us change our economic model, move towards a sustainable model and create jobs. the report said that innovation is not only a product, process or service, but also a movement. so let us be innovators here today. let us start a movement: a movement for innovation.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, my country, the netherlands, is the world champion. unfortunately not in football, which we failed to do last year, but the netherlands is world champion in the use of antibiotics in livestock farming. no country in the world uses as many antibiotics in livestock farming as the netherlands. this has very big implications, not just the dutch practice; in europe, as other colleagues have already said, 25 000 people die every year from bacterial infections. it enters humans through the flesh. 90% of chicken meat in the netherlands contains resistant esbl bacteria, and last month it was even reported that resistant bacteria were found in vegetables, which are transmitted through manure and soil. mr president, this really needs to change. a country like denmark has shown that it is possible, that with good, strict measures with much less antibiotics, to maintain a healthy and strong livestock sector. the european commission has already indicated, and will do so again tonight, that it will come forward with further measures in november. more concrete results will follow in 2012. i would call on the commission to give this problem a real high degree of urgency and not to leave it to the member states who are too slow to take the right measures.
rapporteur. − mr president, before highlighting a few elements of this elaborate report on the role of culture in the eu’s external actions, i want to sincerely thank my colleagues from the committee on culture and education for their constructive collaboration and for unanimously adopting our joint work in the committee vote. of course civil society and other stakeholders have also provided very helpful input. culture has intrinsic value in our liberal democracies. it enriches peoples’ lives. the eu is well known for its cultural diversity and at the same time it is a community of values which apply equally to each citizen. these european values, such as respect for human rights, democracy and fundamental freedoms, are also represented by our cultural products. cultural identity, values and the eu’s position on the global stage are intertwined. european interests are served when cultural aspects are strategically devised through cooperation and partnership, both through cultural programmes and when cultural aspects form an integral part of economic, foreign and security and development policies. through the sharing of literature, film, music and heritage, doors of understanding are opened and bridges between people are built. the eu also has important experiences to share when it comes to overcoming conflict and building stability through shared interests and mutual understanding. in the development of the external action service it is important to mainstream and streamline the role culture has, and should have, in the eu’s external actions. it should be a vital and horizontally integrated element among the broad spectrum of external policies which make up the eu’s foreign policy, from trade relations to its enlargement and neighbourhood policy and its development cooperation and common foreign and security policy. culture also has economic value. europe’s cultural industries contribute to european entrepreneurship, innovation and business, and the eu’s diverse cultural landscape makes it the most attractive tourist destination in the world. knowledge and international skills are crucial to education and employment, as indicated in the eu 2020 strategy, but culture can also be considered as a vehicle which helps to foster democratisation, freedom of expression, inclusion, development, education, reconciliation and much else. this very wide variety of aspects of cultural relations vis-à-vis third countries has led to a fragmentation of policies, which needs to change to a more coordinated and coherent eu strategy. we have chosen to emphasise the organisational and policy frameworks that are needed for the optimal coordination of culture in the eu’s external actions. the filling in of such content should not be governed and regulated to too great an extent ‘from the top down’. european citizens are best able to benefit if the eu acts as a global player on the world stage. this requires funds to be used more efficiently, and we have to be aware of europe’s competitive position and the struggle to attract tourists, talent, artists, business and students. we must speak with one voice and be aware of the competition from cultural programmes by china and the united states, to name just two. but there is no need to reinvent the wheel. the eu has a number of best practices from the member states, as well as from different cultural institutions such as the alliance française and the british council. another aspect that is very prominent in our 21st century is the ever-larger role played by the new technologies, both in culture and in international relations. people depend increasingly on the internet for access to information and can only express themselves freely when this information, and their communications, are not censored. the right to cultural development and other fundamental rights is increasingly facilitated by these technologies. access to cultural content happens through new media as well, and the opportunities for global connectivity around european cultural goods and content should be celebrated and facilitated, for example through europeana or websites of museums and festivals. it is important that the eu develop a strategy for internet freedom. i will come back to some of the concrete recommendations of this report in the last part of my intervention.
mr president, ‘if you want to know where hell is, ask the artist, and if you cannot find the artist you know you are in hell’. i think this quote, which i found by an anonymous author in sarajevo, summarises the importance of arts and culture not only for open societies but also for our relations with the rest of the world. bosnia and herzegovina, with sarajevo as its capital, belongs to one of the black holes on the european map, and this is due to our history. now, however, we have to look to the future. the people of sarajevo have come close to knowing hell, especially during the siege of that city, and arts and culture have helped people there to survive. the orchestra rehearsed while the city was being shelled, and the winter festival full of arts and culture continued. saravejo deserves a european spring. the next generation suffers from the wounds of history, and we have an important responsibility as europeans to look to the future with them and to include them in the european horizon. i fully support ms pack’s initiative and i want to ask parliament to support it and to do everything we can to include sarajevo as a european cultural capital. i believe that, if we all want this, it absolutely can be possible, because it would be very disappointing if our ideas and commonsense were to be blocked by bureaucracy.
mr president, in 2014 it will be 100 years since the murder of archduke franz ferdinand, which triggered the start of the first world war. it was also in bosnia and herzegovina that we had a bloody war in europe in the 1990s. it is important to mark those tragic events, which led to many deaths, suffering, division and horrors, but it is even more important to mark and celebrate european unity and peace today. sarajevo is a city which, despite everything that happened, has maintained its multicultural spirit and strength. in order to recognise this, it would be a strong symbolic gesture to name sarajevo european capital of culture 2014, to reaffirm europe’s message of peace and stability. it is a pity to miss out on this historic momentum by turning to the rules. it is only when we deal with our past, when we recognise and face history, that we can look openly to the future, so please give sarajevo this possibility.
rapporteur. − mr president, i am coming back to you with some concrete next steps on the report on the role of culture in the eu’s external actions that we talked about this morning. i want to start with a quote from a hungarian diplomat in the united states, mr simonyi, who said that ‘rock and roll, culturally speaking, was a decisive element in loosening up communist societies and bringing them closer to the world of freedom’. when we look, in particular, at today’s uprisings of the young generation in north africa and the middle east, we can see that, today, an open internet is that decisive element for moving into the world of freedom. we need an internet freedom strategy to facilitate free expression, press freedom, access to information and access to cultural and educational content. this is a priority, but there are many more concrete suggestions in the report, for which the foundations are already laid down in both the lisbon treaty and the ratification of the unesco conventions. they now need practical implementation. the external action service should coordinate the work of different directorates-general, and create a directorate-general for cultural and digital diplomacy. eeas staff should be trained, and a cultural attaché is needed in each eu representation. there needs to be coordination, streamlining and mainstreaming through an interinstitutional taskforce which should report back to the european parliament. we ask the commission to adopt a green paper in 2011, followed by a communication on a strategy for cultural cooperation in the eu’s external actions. we also call for capacity-building through the funding of initiatives independent of government, and we want to promote eu cultural activities in the rest of the world on line also. existing programmes, such as the european neighbourhood policy and the european instrument for democracy and human rights, have cultural components which need to be coordinated and strategically deployed. we also need to protect and promote cultural heritage, such as through the blue shield programme, and we need to engage in cultural policy dialogues with third countries. human rights should be respected, and cultural arguments can never be used to justify violations of human rights. i would recommend that colleagues read the report. i think that this debate shows that we need many more discussions on culture in the eu.
author. − mr president, while we welcome the release from prison of adnan hajizade and emin milli, the good news about azerbaijan ends about there. the european parliament is very concerned about the massive crackdown on freedom of expression and assembly carried out in azerbaijan following the peaceful protest against the government in march and april of this year. young people, civil society activists, media professionals and opposition politicians are being harassed and intimidated. some demonstrators have been sentenced in mass trials which took place late at night and while people did not have access to a lawyer. most of these lawyers, in turn, were not informed of the locations where the trials were being held. the human rights house of azerbaijan has been closed down by order of the ministry of justice. this climate of fear and intimidation and the breaches of human rights must end. the azerbaijani government is losing its credibility by breaching conventions it has itself signed up to, such as the council of europe and the european convention on human rights. we want president aliyev to keep his word. europe also loses credibility if we do not act upon these breaches. they should have real consequences for the relations between the eu and azerbaijan.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr leinen, for his report. after many years of pushing and pulling, we finally seem to be taking that step towards sustainable national accounts and that is sorely needed, because let's be very honest the economic system we have right now simply doesn't work. the damage to the environment and nature can still be done at virtually no cost. so the system is broken. those costs that nobody pays, they're passed on to society. why is renewable energy more expensive than conventional energy? because the social costs of the loss of coal and gas and oil are not paid by anyone. this may seem like a very technical report, but in the end, it's about really important things. we say in english that measuring is knowing, and that is what this is all about. these european environmental economic accounts are necessary for the transition to a truly sustainable economy. i would like to ask the commission and the member states to do something about the data that is being collected. measurement is knowing, that's very important, but using the knowledge we gain from that is much more important, and that's the essential next step. i very much hope that this will lead, for example, to the elimination of the hundreds of billions in subsidies which are still having a detrimental effect on nature and the environment in the world. i would like to thank the committee and our rapporteur once again. this is just the beginning, however. and then let's really take the steps that the knowledge that this brings makes possible.

president, rapporteur, commissioner, it is good to look back now and then, especially in the middle of such a period and just before the adoption of the financial framework and the legislative tasks of parliament. what is striking is that scientific research in europe is of the highest quality. the problem is to turn it into products and organize the whole chain. of course, it doesn't make much sense if we generate a huge number of patents, sell them around the world and if the added value of that goes to other parts of the world. we need to be so restrained, so trained, that the whole process happens here. a second point: on the one hand, it is about excellence, but on the other hand, excellence must stand on two legs and not be free floating intelligent. excellence must take up posts in regions and cities. it cannot be that you have this high added value only in metropolitan regions. it is our task as a parliament to combine what research produces with what it can do in terms of innovation. you have formulated your own innovation union, very good initiatives, and then we can use those various other funds, such as the structural funds, for that. we have to apply conditionality in the coming years. in this sense, we must also ensure that regions which are lagging behind invest in specialisation. it is possible to do so, not only with european money, but with the member state's own money. this is how you create a single common agenda and in this context, tools such as joint programming are also of great importance. two more little dots. firstly, we must certainly continue with risk-taking facilities. this initiative, the eib's risk charging finance facility, has worked extremely well. and secondly, of course, mrs cavalho, you did very well. there's less red tape. now, give room to those jtis, where the corporate sector is 50% involved. and we're treating it like it's an official organization! president, commissioner, we are in the midst of a crisis and frankly we have more sensible things to do at the moment than point fingers and assign blame. when you're in the middle of a crisis, there's nothing to do but bite your tongue and solve the crisis first. we can discuss all other matters afterwards. the first priority is to find a treatment for the patients against this antibiotic-resistant bacteria and to trace the source. we must also, above all, communicate very well to draw the fine line between information for patients, for consumers and for producers and to do justice to everyone. the question is: what about our crisis management? i'm sure it's not the last crisis. who's directing? there are too many authorities involved and someone needs to take the lead. that is what the council is going to have to discuss with you this afternoon. how is the future of directing going and who will take it over? the use of antibiotics is indeed a thorn in the side of animals and humans. there are too many differences between countries. and last but not least, the damage must be repaired. clarity and purpose.
the eurovignette directive is important because it sets the european rules for a kilometre charge and thus also ensures a level playing field in the european union for road transport. i do not wish to conceal the fact that our group had serious problems with the original committee proposal and also with the position taken by the rapporteur at first reading. fortunately, the rapporteur has taken strong steps in our direction, and it is certainly also thanks to the council and the hungarian presidency, which have managed to achieve not only a solid majority in the council but also to get this parliament to support this position. the majority of my group will support this agreement too, because it is the first step towards introducing the polluter pays principle, but in a sensible way. the first is that there must be incentives to promote sustainability, not just to increase burdens. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this will keep the total toll charge the same. the exceptions for euro v and euro vi are also a positive point, and the fact that the member states have again committed themselves to regulating the interoperability of toll systems is a thorn in our side, because that is still not the case. the earmark is also important for parliament. we wanted more. but it is certainly an important step, not only the percentage now allocated but also the reporting obligation for the member states. i hope that is a prelude to further consideration of these funds.
the present proposal for the eurovignette, mr president, amounts to the fact that efficient european road transport subsidizes inefficient railways. the most worrying thing is that this parliament regards the present proposal as only a first step. this first step, which is obviously in the wrong direction, means that the member states' current right to implement this legislation will soon become an obligation, that the tax per kilometre will only increase and, above all, that it will be the driver's turn. this parliament is talking about a single system between member states. the introduction of this proposal, however, means that transport companies can prepare themselves for a new cabin in their lorry, with new taxes, of course. it is clear that with this proposal the european parliament is turning the neck of road transport over to climate hysteria. the transport sector is already suffering enough and is not waiting for more taxes imposed by europe.
the commission has already taken a number of important decisions in this area, and i am sure that the council will be able to take a decision on this matter in due course. finally, we have now agreed to pass on the costs of using infrastructure, noise and air pollution to the carrier. i hope that many european member states will adopt this system, including the netherlands. the rutten government is wise to bring the plans for 'paying others for mobility' out of the closet. those plans are in line with the current agreement. i hope that the european commission will come forward with proposals to extend these costs to other modes of transport in stages. of course, this must take account of the specific characteristics of each sector. inland waterway transport is not the only user of river water. we see that these days with this extreme drought very clearly. agriculture has now opened its sprinklers wide. but we've taken an important step and we have to move on.

it is interesting to note that whenever we talk about a budget for the future, the debate is always about 'how much exactly'. the debate should really be about three issues, three priorities which are also crucial for us greens. firstly, we need a vision of europe, and what is the added value of the european budget for that vision of europe? secondly, yes, mr ashworth, the budget must be renewed and greener, it must be a budget for the future. but a budget for the future also means changes and still means european money. the third priority concerns the eu's own resources. make the system fairer and more transparent, and we'll talk about own revenue. let me briefly review these three priorities, starting with added value. here again the debate is as if the money going to brussels disappeared into a black hole. what nonsense, really total nonsense! if you look at what can be done at the european level, there are many reasons to spend money at the european level and not at the national level. now, for example, diplomatic services, embassies abroad. do we want 27 in all kinds of countries in africa, or do we want one from the eu? that means more money going to the eu for that eu embassy, but that means 27 times less money being spent in 27 member states. that's efficient use of money. look at the infrastructure. if we as countries build infrastructure, how do we ensure that this is also true across borders in the field of energy and networks? you need european money. or take innovation and research. get this put together, so you can do that right together, so you have economies of scale. that's a smart investment. that is a vision of the added value of europe. and that is why we need a european budget. secondly, the budget must be forward-looking. the current budget is too much business as usual. we have 2020 targets for a smart economy, a sustainable economy. so that's where we need to invest. the challenges facing the eu are dealing with scarcity, scarcity of natural resources, but also of labour. the eu must invest in this so that we can deal with it. the agricultural fund must be renewed and greenered so that agriculture becomes a sector which manages natural resources well and efficiently. the structural funds must also respond to the new challenges, such as energy saving. ensure that the structural funds contribute to the objectives we set for 2020. that's the innovation we should be talking about. what are the new challenges? sustainable energy - that means infrastructure, and we need money for that - foreign policy - the arab spring: what is the european response? we need european money for this too - poverty reduction inside and outside the eu, climate change, innovation, all reasons to act at eu level. the third point concerns own resources. the current system is bankrupt. the rebates that the british have, that the dutch and the danes want, it's a bankrupt system. make it fairer, make it more transparent, or provide own revenue for the eu. think of it as a financial transaction tax. then you have a fair system where the money goes to the eu and we can achieve our own goals. that's smart policy. it must be different, but it must certainly also be at european level. finally, i would like to ask the member states who say that they can do less and less, but who at the same time give the eu all kinds of new priorities: if you want less, what priorities are we going to surrender as the eu? because if you want less, you have to make choices. so far, we've heard that there are more and more priorities, with less money. that math is a mess. that should be the question to the british and dutch governments.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - mr president, for the committee on development and cooperation this is not only a clear report but also an essential one, because it establishes that the european union is aware of its responsibility for the poorest countries for the next seven years and is therefore making a contribution to the budget. it states that member states must fulfil their own commitments. 0.7% of gross national income for development cooperation, they are a long way from that. but cooperation within the european union for development cooperation leads to savings, to avoid duplication, to better and more efficient spending. the report states that measures to combat climate change should not be financed from the development budget. it calls for greater priority to be given to strengthening human rights, democracy and the rule of law. it calls for new methods of financing in addition to grants, combinations of loans and grants. it stressed that humanitarian action must retain its own independent financing instrument, since emergency aid must be neutral, independent of political choices in external action, in the eeas (european external action service). with these choices, the european union remains the most important partner for the developing countries of the world, and that is essential.

in writing. - i cannot support the garriga polledo report. the reason for this is that i am opposed in principle to the replacement of member state contributions by direct eu funds, read an eu tax. the union must be at the service of the member states and this must be reflected in the financing. in substance, i have difficulty with the eu 2020 strategy as a guide for the multiannual financial framework. the objectives of the strategy concern social and employment policies, which are the responsibility of the member states. the failure of the lisbon strategy shows what happens when we base our strategy on matters over which the european union has no control. the report also seeks to reduce the amount of arrears by increasing payments. however, if the implementation of projects is slow, it is rational to take fewer new contractual commitments. in short, the report shows no sign of any sense of prudent budgetary management in the face of the enormous economic problems facing the european economies. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. in this way we are repeating at european level the mistakes made by a number of member states in recent years.

is it a matter of hungary alone? i'd say not at all. the state of democracy in one member state in europe affects the other member states. if one country chooses to establish an autocracy through fair elections and a two-thirds majority, the rest may be shocked and the rest may try to stop it. i quote györgy konrád, who says: "in hungary, a so-called majority has succeeded in democratically creating an autocratic regime". and that's the truth. because if you are now pushing a law through parliament in hungary and say that it can only be changed with a two-thirds majority from now on, you know that it will not be easy to change it again after the next elections. that kind of thing hollows out the center of an election. it is the right of a population to say every four years 'i liked this, i did not like this', and then to be able to make changes. but when a democratically elected regime organizes it in such a way that you can't make any changes after four years, that's unhealthy and wrong. if we in europe look the other way and say 'it is hungary's business', if 26 member states are not prepared to say 'friends, this is not how we treat each other in our society', and if the european commission says the same, what do we have to say to countries where the situation is much worse? what should we say to hungarians who are not in the two-thirds majority? i'm sorry, but even after the next two elections, you have no chance of rolling back laws. it is a pity that we in europe are all looking the other way and not acting.

the european arrest warrant is a matter of human destiny, human rights and fundamental freedoms. in the netherlands, there is a lot of attention to the fate of cor disselkoen. in 1997, this dutch entrepreneur was accused of violating polish tax rules. he was imprisoned for two months under miserable conditions. after a very high bail, he was released and for 12 years he heard nothing more about the case. last year, poland suddenly demanded the surrender of the entrepreneur. poland made use of the european arrest warrant. a week and a half ago, he was transported to poland. according to disselkoen's lawyer, he was held in a prison as filthy and overcrowded as it was in 1997. the conversation between disselkoen and his lawyer was recorded by a video camera against all rules. in addition, four new charges were suddenly brought against him. the judge even threatened that these could lead to a new arrest warrant. disselkoen has paid another large bail and is back in the netherlands. but his case shows that the european arrest warrant can lead to dehumanizing situations. the commission's report shows that it is aware of the problems and is concerned about them. that is why my group has placed its hopes in the commissioner. the commissioner, can you explain the wording of the proportionality requirements and have the council adopt them? and can you ensure that individuals actually have the right to challenge an arrest warrant in both the issuing and executing member states? do you agree that in countries where the situation in prisons and detention centres is still inhumane, the execution of an arrest warrant can be consistently denied, that there will be a human rights review and that cor disselkoen will not have to fear further misery?
i am very grateful to mrs ludford for her question, because it was burning on my lips. i was present in poland last week at a trial of mr cor disselkoen - his name has already been mentioned here today - a dutchman who was extradited to poland on a european arrest warrant for a case dating back to the mid-1990s. in the few days he was locked up - because a settlement was finally reached between the dutch judge and the polish judge - in those few days, the guards there stole his medication for his heart condition. the problem is not only how the european arrest warrant is being used, but also that the prison conditions in various countries in europe are such that you cannot really send people there anymore. it should also be the discretion of a judge in the requested country to say: i am not going to be extradited to a country where the prison facilities are inhumane, and i am abiding by article 3 of the european convention on human rights as a judge. i agree with the council that countries should be discreet about the matters for which they make such a request, but countries should also have the space to say: that's such a small offense, i don't turn people in for that. it would therefore be very important for poland to adapt its legal system. rapporteur. – madam president, if members look at their e-mails and all the requests from lobbyists and industry, and the pros and cons on the climate, they can conclude that climate is very clearly back on the political agenda. that is a very good thing, because climate has never been off the scientific agenda. if you look at how science has been evolving meanwhile, the arguments for stronger climate policies have become stronger and stronger every day. however, in the political field, it was rather quiet on the climate front after copenhagen and cancún. now it is clearly back on the political agenda. that is a good thing. however, what is very important is that in the meantime it has become a different debate. it is not only a climate argument any more. it has become more and more an economic debate, an economic argument. if you look at the new industries relying on climate policies, they are asking for more ambitious climate policies. or look at our oil dependency and the situation in northern africa: the debate on oil dependency is becoming more and more important for our economic position in the eu. but look too at all the letters from industry, really lobbying for going to 30% because it is in their interest; it helps their profits if we step up our climate ambitions. this very clearly indicates that there is a fundamental shift in the debate on climate policies. that is very important. it is not the economy versus ecology; it is the economy and ecology. that is crucial. so what is in the report? of course, we are asking the commission to come forward with a proposal to move to 25% reduction domestically. this is very important, because that is the way to become less dependent on oil; that is the way to harvest the health co-benefits; that is also the way to really harvest the potential of energy savings. so energy savings are also a crucial part of the report because, if we meet our energy savings targets, we are already on the path of achieving 25% domestically in the eu. in the report, we are asking for 30% overall; so 25% domestically, but 30% as a whole. there are a lot of offsets still in the market, so if you have 25% domestically, we are discussing 30% as a whole as well. that also limits somewhat the role of offsetting, which has also been part of the debate; that is also in the report. this is also a discussion on the adjustment of the emissions trading system. let me be very clear. if we are stepping up climate ambitions through energy savings and we only do something relating to energy savings, the carbon price will go down; studies estimate from eur 15 to eur 0 per tonne carbon. so we also need to do something in the ets system to make sure we have the right price and really have the incentives to move to green technologies. it is going to be a crucial vote tomorrow for a future economy, a green economy. i urge the commission to follow up the request we will make as a parliament tomorrow by coming forward with a legislative proposal. the council is moving, parliament will set out its position tomorrow, and then it is up to the commission to come forward with a proposal. we know when we are discussing a legislative proposal that in the council there will be no veto for any country any more. we will have a majority in parliament, as we will see tomorrow, and a majority in the council to really step up our climate ambitions and make sure that the true future economy is the green economy.

in writing. the pvv votes in favour of the consumer rights directive because it will better protect dutch consumers when shopping online. in the netherlands, consumers enjoy good protection, but when shopping online, often in foreign shops, this protection is not guaranteed or unclear. the present consumer directive improves the protection of crossborder purchases via the internet, and thus adds value for the dutch consumer. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. that's a marked improvement over the current seven days. the pvv also welcomes the fact that this directive will put an end to hidden costs and ex post costs. the final price to be paid by the consumer must be clearly visible in advance. this directive thus contributes to greater protection for dutch consumers and the improvement of the internal market. the pvv fully supports that.
in writing. the vvd is against an absolute increase in the eu budget in 2012. with all member states facing heavy austerity measures on public finances, the eu cannot be left behind, and the eu will also have to make choices about what to do and what not to do.
in writing. − a strong cap with its associated resources is needed to ensure food security. this report shows that there is support for this amongst the people's representatives and thus also amongst the eu citizen. the structure of direct payments: i am fully in favour of maintaining the basic premium. the european food production meets high standards which impose high costs on farmers, but the market does not pay enough for them. the commission and the ep propose greening services for a fee as 'top-ups' to the basic premium. i would stress that under no circumstances should they lead to an increase in the pressure of regulation, but rather should they provide opportunities. the provision of these services will therefore have to be on a voluntary basis. the report also states that the ep is against the commission's proposed obligation to set up 'top-ups'. i urge that when a list of options for greening services is being drawn up, projects that create a win-win situation for the environment and farmer be sought out, leading to further innovation and sustainable growth. an example of this is the conversion of manure into energy. i would stress that the above proposals can only be implemented if the agricultural budget is increased.
the trade in complex financial products, the content of which was no longer understood or the risks of which could not be assessed, played an important role in the run-up to the financial and economic crises. that is why it is good that our commissioner barnier has come forward with these legislative proposals, because we need more openness in the derivatives trade and collateral must be better covered. our rapporteur, werner lange, has therefore done an excellent job of developing these proposals further into an ambitious package, in which we also build on the foundations we have laid for the european supervisory authority. derivatives should be traded publicly, as this gives supervisors greater visibility into the trade flows between buyers and sellers and allows for a better assessment of risks. i am glad that the proposals we are voting on tomorrow also take account of the special nature of pension funds, which are already subject to very strict risk legislation. there is a different aspect to the risk. they are not allowed to speculate and must cover the risk. they also have to meet the requirements of transparency and openness, but not the clearing obligation, and fortunately the exception is well developed in the proposal. a temporary and partial derogation, which allows for a solution to be found on the basis of a few years of practical experience, after which it will be up to the commission to decide whether we have found that solution or whether the time should be extended. i hope that on this and other points, the council, which has not yet been able to reach agreement, will follow parliament so that we can quickly reach a final conclusion on this legislation.
author. − madam president, i am very much looking forward to the answers to these very detailed questions. i would like to start by adding yet another one. we know that south korea and qatar have repeatedly asked to begin negotiations with the european commission for an agreement because they too are requesting the transfer of pnr. so far the commission has turned their request down. that means that there is no legal basis for the transfer of data to those countries. we know that other countries will soon follow suit. i would like to know what the commission’s course of action will be. secondly, i would like to highlight one point from the oral question, and that is the issue of necessity and proportionality. the demonstration that we had two weeks ago in manchester of the use of pnr underlined what we have been saying here for a long time. pnr data can, in particular circumstances, be useful, maybe even necessary, when people cross borders, but the long-term storage of data concerning all passengers remains very problematic. essentially, what is being done is that data is stored because it may come in handy one day in order to solve crimes that have not yet been committed and that we are not aware of. that is not proportional and your own legal advisers and those of the council agree. i look forward to your answer.
mr president, i will be very brief. firstly, i am intrigued to hear about the plans for a multilateral agreement for all the other countries. i am wondering why they get different treatment and when this will start, because some countries are already asking for pnr. secondly, on the legal opinions of the commission and council legal services, they were actually not on minor issues. they went right to the core. they challenged the core: the proportionality and the necessity of long-term storage. i would like to hear a little more about that. thirdly, regarding the legal basis, the european court of justice did not confirm police and justice cooperation as a legal basis; it simply rejected article 95 – internal markets – as a legal basis. finally, commissioner, on ‘push’ and ‘pull’, the parliament’s resolution is crystal clear: ‘push’ only. no ‘pull’: not even a little bit of ‘pull’. it is like being pregnant: you cannot be ‘a little bit’ pregnant. there is no such thing as ‘a little bit’ of ‘pull’. ‘pull’ is giving a log-in to a third country. ‘push’ only!

in writing. − this report aims to ensure that remote rural areas and low-income citizens have access to basic telecommunications services, the so-called universal services. this is necessary, as the report says, to promote social inclusion and social integration. the pvv considers that such social policy should remain a national matter and that european policy has no place in it. the proposal to put broadband in every corner of europe is also absurd. that is like driving across europe and connecting all the villages with four-lane highways. the netherlands has excellent internet connections, and we do not need european policy for that. the pvv also agrees with the importance of the 112 emergency number as a european number to call in case of emergencies. it is the responsibility of the member states to make this public. member states are best placed to choose the instruments with which to increase the awareness of the 112 number among the public. the pvv will therefore not support the proposal for a european policy in this area.
in writing. − the pvv supports this initiative to improve cooperation between national public authorities responsible for enforcing consumer legislation. the interests of consumers in the european single market are increasingly transnational. it is therefore necessary that national authorities cooperate effectively when consumers are duped by sellers and service providers abroad. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are not misled by fraudulent sellers and service providers abroad. this requires a good exchange of information between the authorities of the various member states. the report calls on the european commission to make proposals to improve operational cooperation between national authorities to better protect consumers in cross-border purchases. the pvv supports this objective as long as it is based on cooperation and exchange of information between national authorities.
in writing. − i have neither voted for nor against this report on cohesion policy after 2013. on the one hand, the report makes good proposals for improving the effectiveness of spending, for example in terms of reducing administrative burdens, financial control and a more results-oriented approach to policy by setting objectives and indicators in advance. however, i think that the extension of the line for the future should also have been the moment to critically consider whether all the money flows involved should necessarily pass through the eu. i am of the opinion that cohesion policy and its funds should be limited to the poorest regions in the least prosperous member states and that the rich member states should in principle not be eligible for the funds (except for projects under objective 3, where eu action has added value in bringing about crossborder territorial cooperation). the richer member states should bear less of the burden of the policy's consolidation. they could then spend the money on areas in their country that need extra care, without this flow of money going through the eu.

mr president, as you have already noted, we are dealing with a sensitive subject which is also being discussed in the groups, namely the possible re-authorisation of animal meal. the commission has also proposed that the use of animal meal be restricted to certain areas of the community. of course, the re-authorisation of such animal meal must never again lead to such abuses. that is why we are talking only about feeding non-ruminants, not of course animal meal of our own species, and about control, because now - and this is the big difference with the discussion of a few years ago - there are tests that can distinguish between the types of animal meal we are actually talking about. under these conditions, the partial lifting of the ban on animal meal is justified. not only justified, but desirable. from an animal welfare point of view, it is desirable, because chickens and pigs are omnivores and on a plant-based diet alone they actually get suboptimal nutrition. good animal protein is used in such a case, instead of being thrown away or misused as it is at present. the eu is only 30% self-sufficient in protein crops for animal feed. we have to import 42 million tons a year. the reuse of animal meal can hopefully reduce that dependence a little bit. in short, support for the european commission's approach, but only under the right conditions and under strict control.

the party for freedom is in favour of improving road safety, but we do not see any intention of using personal data at european level to collect fines. the collection of fines is a national matter and where the need exists, countries can already work together bilaterally to tackle notorious speedsters. that is why we certainly do not give carte blanche to any country to randomly request privacy-sensitive personal data from dutch citizens. we, the party for freedom, are therefore opposed to this proposal. if it turns out that in practice there are disproportionately many motorists from another member state committing road traffic offences in one member state, those member states may conclude bilateral agreements, as is already the case.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs. - mr president, as rapporteur for the committee on civil liberties i want to send a clear message. there are three criteria that body scanners must meet. the freedom of choice (you can't be forced to go through that machine), the storage of data (no storage of data and no need to take pictures of people that security personnel are looking at in little rooms down the street) and no x-rays. the latter is crucial to the health of the european. these devices aren't just at airports. for example, these devices are also in the courthouse in paris, and i am sure they will be in many more places. that is why the european parliament and the european commission must set out clear rights for europeans and clear criteria to be met by these machines. we must not listen to the commercial that says: "we're making devices that work with x-rays". no, we have rules for that. i am glad to see that the european commission is now proposing legislation to ban x-rays and to guarantee that freedom of choice. i would also like to know what the data storage and the photographs are. what i am very disappointed about is that you still choose to implement this decision as a kind of technical decision in comitology and that you come with proposals before we have even had a chance to vote on the european parliament's involvement. i would expect you to give us full consent because this is not just a technical decision. this is about security, but it's also about the privacy of citizens. the debate about whether these are machines we really need, and whether this will bring security to airports, that's a debate i'd like to have with you. what i am concerned about now is that if people think it is necessary, it should be done properly, but i have my doubts about the necessity.

first of all, mr president, i would like to congratulate the beautiful country of denmark on its great decision to re-introduce border controls. it shows the resolve of a sovereign state within an increasingly intrusive european union which does not accept that a foreign pseudo-power should determine the dangers facing its citizens. crime is crossing borders. it is an illusion to fight it by removing borders. the removal of frontiers creates an oil spill effect whereby crime flows from eastern european member states to the rich western european countries. the reintroduction of border controls is a proven means of a sovereign state to prevent undesirable elements from crossing national borders. a sovereign country has an obligation to itself at all times to defend the interests of its own people in the best possible way. a government that neglects its people at the expense of bandits and bums, ignores its own history and denies the interests of its citizens. whether countries want to introduce effective border controls should be their own choice. the european bureaucrats are currently banning effective border controls. for example, dutch customs officers are required to check only two trains per day and only two train sets per train. of course, enthusiasm for the free movement of persons in the eu member states is steadily declining. let our customs and marshals have the freedom to control as they wish, based on years of experience, empirical research and a sense of urgency. if the european project is really as perfect as the european elite would have us believe, then we won't even need it. in writing. − i voted in favour of compromise amendment no 53 to the kastler report ‘on the european year for active ageing and solidarity between generations (2012)’, since i fully support the idea of raising awareness of the consequences of population ageing for society, and putting the issue on the political agenda. with regard to eu policies, i strongly believe that policy makers should pay more attention to the issues and challenges that result from population ageing, regardless of the policy field. population ageing should indeed be treated from an overarching and holistic perspective. the consequences of population ageing are too often overlooked or even ignored, in particular those with regard to social security, pension acquirement and eligibility, and (long term) care provision. since the kastler report does not offer effective and long-term solutions with regard to guaranteeing, maintaining, and improving social security – in particular pensions and care provisions – i abstained in the final vote.

the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. - mr president, commissioner, rapporteur, thank you for the paper and your strategies. and the other colleagues, thank you for all your input. i think we have a very useful piece here. i think, frankly, it could go a lot further. it's a very important issue. what is not seen when we look at the current economic and financial crisis is that behind it is already the next crisis coming up that we can prevent with a good strategy right now. it's about the scarcity of raw materials, which all emerging economies naturally want, and which is already putting pressure on our future growth after this crisis. what i really like is that there is a lot of attention to this vulnerability of the eu, to our dependence. and it's also good that we focus on our own strength. how do we deal with our own waste? what can we do internally in europe? the call for innovation, which is essential to recycle the raw materials that we have managed to preserve on our own soil. it's just that the innovation has yet to get off the ground. it's not concrete enough yet. what exactly do we mean by that? where is the real paradigm shift that europe is crying out for, especially in response to this financial and economic crisis and in response to the dependence that we have in so many ways? we need a change in behaviour, in industry and among the public. how can we do that? we need a completely different economic model. and i'm not just talking about the governance structures in the banks or in the government. no, i'm talking about really intervening deeply in this economy: for example, a lease-based society, where all products are returned automatically; it is a shift in the tax burden. encouraging, for example, just the little bit needed to buy a better quality, longer lasting, more environmentally friendly product; much stricter ecodesign guidelines. i particularly appreciate your words when you say that we must do everything with respect for nature. because that's very, very important. we often too readily focus on those empty areas, those natural areas which could still be used for mining. but who's to say we don't live on a gold mine in the city? yeah, we're already living on a gold mine of garbage, our urban mining. but maybe we do live on some source of something we can use. so let's look at those areas, and not just the empty ones. all colleagues have put forward good proposals, and i hope they will be implemented, such as the quality label, the chain of custody, chain liability, the good social quality label, attention to illegal shipments from europe and, above all, a lot of innovation.
i am very pleased that the commission has taken this initiative. and thank you, commissioner tajani, for your comments on this report. it seems like everyone's on the same page. a great strategy to secure raw materials. perhaps i should be a little more specific, because when i compare this report with the original commission report, there are indeed very big differences. i am glad that mr tajani is now happy with the european parliament's report, but this is really a different strategy. i read the commission's report and it is mainly about a struggle with china to get raw materials to europe as quickly as possible. if that doesn't work, then we can mine in europe. and now part three: oh, yeah, we should be a little more efficient with the raw materials, too. that is exactly the wrong order, and this report shows that. we must first of all manage our natural resources, our raw materials, more efficiently. a very simple example is recycling. if we mine one ton, on average, we'll probably find five grams of gold in there. if we look at one ton of electronic waste, there's 150 grams of gold in there. it's a much smarter, cheaper, more efficient way to get our raw materials. it is also - and you are the commissioner for industry - a european industry which must be encouraged, here in europe, to reuse our own raw materials which are already on the market here. that's smart policy. if we are going to mine, it will be in natura 2000 areas. this report makes that very clear. you say that we must take the environment into account. i want to see that more clearly. so we do not want to touch natura 2000 areas. but i also do not want europe to go to the north pole, for example. at least we'll be talking about concrete policy instead of vague words. thirdly: if we do have to import raw materials from outside europe, then it is the contact with developing countries that is at issue. then we can talk about diplomacy. but i would like to see this policy reflected throughout the commission, including the commissioner for trade, mr de gucht. the european union is not a political force, but a political force.
mr president, access to raw materials is of crucial importance to the well-being of european citizens and the situation on the raw materials markets therefore deserves the highest priority. in this context, the eu must ensure transparency, stability and a certain degree of market regulation, always asking how far regulation can go. in addition, the potential of recycling and diversification of sources must be exploited. sustainability is the key word here. european companies must have a responsible purchasing policy, but the european union must ensure that there is a good framework. when the eu proposes legislation on conflict minerals, it is imperative that the ordinary people involved in mineral extraction are not the ones who suffer. the need for raw materials must be met in a way that does justice to the countries of origin in terms of human rights, the environment, fair and open trade and stable markets. it goes without saying that i have great respect and esteem for our rapporteur.
mr president, a great deal has been said about the importance of an effective raw materials strategy for europe. i'd like to make it a little bit more concrete, so i'll take one raw material: phosphate. it's important for the growth of crops. however, in 50 to 90 years, the world's phosphate reserves will be exhausted. and that's while in the same period of time, the world's population grows to nine billion people, who also need to eat. we can continue to talk about this, mr commissioner, but we can also take action, for example by encouraging fertilisation here in the european union. firstly, it produces renewable energy; secondly, it reduces the environmental burden; and thirdly, it produces as a final product a valuable mineral concentrate, also known as digestate, which can replace fertiliser, and thus phosphate. i think that's a win-win situation. you can encourage this innovation by supporting such projects through, for example, the structural funds, rural policy or the greening component of the new common agricultural policy. and also by defining the mineral concentrate as a replacement for fertiliser and no longer, as is the case now, as a primary fertiliser. this is a technical story, but this example of phosphate shows that an effective resource strategy only has a chance if words are translated into concrete actions, however technical they may be. i hope the commissioner is prepared to be as specific as he is and i would like to hear from him whether he will take these steps in the future.
mr president, i had a few more afterburners. i would like to know more about the difference between the commission proposal and the ep proposal, which you say you welcome. that's very nice. but what are the practical measures? we have talked about stockpiling, and frankly, that does not seem to be the solution. i hope you will not take the lead in this, but perhaps you can give a concrete answer. there is a strong focus on purely industrial materials, and there has been a lot of talk about phosphate. i'm all for that. that's very important. how will you ensure that attention is paid to this and to innovation? the original efficiency targets were set, but they've all been taken out. i would like to see an efficiency target similar to the energy targets. we had called for a ban on dumping; that has now been moderated. but i would also like to see that as far as waste is concerned - which you also consider very important - that we do indeed remain on top of that waste in order to be able to process it. so we need to have a landfill ban so that all that material can be put directly into the recycling line. how's the stamp coming? and, last but not least, those tax measures we've been talking about. who will take the initiative? will the eib play a role? and besides, who's gonna coordinate all this? you or mr potočnik with his resource efficiency flagship?
rapporteur. − mr president, i had actually prepared a rather different intervention for today, but things seem to be developing very rapidly. first of all, i think it is regrettable that we are not having a full debate. this is a very important topic. i think this is underlined by the controversy about the report, and therefore i think it would have merited the presence of the responsible commissioner, the council and many colleagues, including the shadows. i am rather unpleasantly surprised by the extreme reluctance – both within the european parliament and the european commission – to evaluate the eu counter-terrorism policies. as a democrat, i think evaluation is an essential part of democracy. it is about transparency, accountability to our citizens and making our policies better and more efficient. i think that a democracy that is unable and unwilling to take a critical look at its own policies is not worth the term ‘democracy’. if we are convinced that we have got it right in our policies, i think we can be confident that they will stand the test. we do not only have a duty to our own citizens, we also have to be a moral compass in the world. frankly, colleagues, i feel that if we are unable to carry out a thorough evaluation of the measures we have taken over the last ten years, we lose all credibility and moral authority, and that is particularly important at the time of the arab spring. how can we look the young people in north africa in the eye and say to them – with credibility – you have to create democracy and the rule of law, respect human rights and live by the principles of accountability and transparency, if we do not live up to that and are unwilling to do it ourselves? i have to say that there is a great deal of controversy around this report; i think this is very regrettable and disappointing. i did expect a controversial debate, but i did not expect it to be quite this acrimonious. we voted in july, but ever since the vote, several members and groups have changed their positions, in some cases by 180 degrees. texts that were agreed unanimously are now being put forward for deletion by several groups. i find that surprising. now let us have a look at the texts that are so incredibly shocking and radical that they have to be taken out. first there is a paragraph – paragraph 26 – that was agreed unanimously (i underline unanimously) by all the political groups, referring to article 70 of the treaty – very shocking, very radical – calling for an evaluation on the basis of that same treaty. there is, for example, a paragraph saying that we should not only address the consequences of terrorism but also the causes; very radical. there is a paragraph calling for impact assessments and proportionality tests; very radical – except it is already compulsory. there is another paragraph referring to proper access to documents: that is the official position of this house! so i have to say i am very surprised by some of the requests for separate votes. however, i do think that in this case – and i will conclude on that, chair – it is much more important that we achieve a result. i will try until the very last moment to bring people together and see if we can arrive at some sort of agreement, because we owe it not to this house, but to our citizens and to the rest of the world.

rapporteur. − mr president, with reference to rule 177 of the rules of procedure, i would like to propose to the house that we postpone the vote on the evaluation of eu counter-terrorism policies. although the report was adopted by the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs in july, it would seem that positions have been shifting in recent days and controversy has arisen over a number of issues. that is very unfortunate considering that the issue is very sensitive. as rapporteur, i worked on a timetable which would have allowed for a plenary vote before the summer break, but the timetable has been slipping. however, what counts in the end is the result; evaluation is a key element of transparency and accountability, and it is important that parliament has a clear and consensual view of what evaluation should include. i believe that with a little more time we may find circumstances more favourable to a common approach and be able to present a result that will command widespread support. i therefore ask the house to support postponement.
in writing. − as there are a number of civilian products and technologies which can also be used for military and terrorist purposes, it is necessary to monitor the export of these products. in order to ensure that this monitoring is carried out properly, a common list of products for which an export licence is to be applied for has been drawn up. the main products concerned are chemicals and building materials. the pvv voted in favour of this proposal because different lists are currently being worked on in different european countries. a common framework should prevent potential buyers from exploiting gaps between member states and 'shopping' in different countries to purchase these products. the additional advantage of a common framework is clarity for dutch exporting companies and fair competition through equal rules for exporting companies from the different countries in europe.
in writing. the pvv is against control and interference from brussels over investment decisions of national credit institutions. national member states are responsible for this. in the netherlands, for example, the 'nederlandse kredietverzekering maatschappij' (dutch credit insurance company) provides loans to dutch businesses. in addition, international agreements have already been concluded: oecd standards are designed to prevent unlawful subsidies and promote fair competition. europe is only too keen to have a hand in imposing additional obligations on export credit institutions in areas such as social and environmental protection. however, if europe imposes such redundant standards, the competitiveness of european industry will be seriously weakened. companies and institutions from other oecd countries, such as the us, canada and turkey, do not have to meet the absurd climate targets that europe is imposing on itself.
in writing. - this report is your european charter. it includes plans for: special grants for women ́; women-friendly financial services; provide basic courses for girls; an eu campaign to promote women's participation in work. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of women are respected. in the document, the european parliament has also been free to point fingers at specific countries (poland) and the situation of specific groups such as the roma. at the same time, not a word is said about the abominable position of a significant proportion of migrant women in muslim circles. the netherlands is not waiting for brussels to interfere in social policy, that is a task for the member states themselves, not for the eu. the netherlands has been pursuing its own emancipation policy for years with projects such as the 'talent programme' and 'eigen kracht', and the netherlands is doing well in the european context. this proposal is absurd in its content and completely unnecessary. the pvv therefore votes against the whole proposal.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, the credit for this report does not belong to me, of course, but to heidi hautala, my former colleague in the group, who is now in the finnish government. in her the finnish government has got a very good minister, but our parliament has lost a champion of openness. it's a privilege to be allowed to take over this wand from her. the hautala report, now the sargentini report, was adopted by the committee on civil liberties in june. this was done by an overwhelming majority, based on a shared awareness that there is still a great deal of transparency in european decision-making. the reflex of secrecy is still very often present, whereas openness should be the starting point. too often citizens themselves have to ask for access to documents that could have been online long ago. too often people still look for information on european websites, whereas all the documents that shed light on decision-making on a subject could also be clearly grouped together. you and i all know what that means for us, too. too often the court and the ombudsman have to call the european institutions to order. and this is despite the fact that the treaty of lisbon obliges us more than ever to make decisions as openly as possible. access to documents and good administration are fundamental rights to which the institutions, bodies and agencies of the european union have been effectively bound since lisbon. it is therefore not helpful, or rather harmful, to european credibility if the council keeps secret documents on its involvement in the legislative process. it is therefore surprising that the commission is still proposing to restrict the definition of 'documents', which would make it easier to keep internal documents under wraps. this secrecy is contrary to the treaty of lisbon. the present resolution therefore calls on the commission to submit a new proposal for a revision of the regulation on access to documents, a proposal which is therefore 'lisbon-proof'. i would like to hear the commissioner's reaction to that. but for greater openness, our parliament does not have to wait for other institutions. we can do it ourselves, as this resolution - hautala/sargentini - says. today, trialogue and mediation are the most opaque part of the legislative procedure, whereas these negotiations are usually decisive for the final outcome. the relevant documents should therefore be published by parliament. this is essential for the press, for civil society and for the interested citizen. they have the right to follow the legislative process closely. for the same reason, parliament's committees should have at least one orientation vote before proceeding to a trialogue with the council and the commission. that should be made mandatory in our rules of procedure. finally, the debt crisis is putting a heavy strain on confidence in the european union. the institutions and the member states are advised not to break any more promises. we must keep the promise of maximum openness, and time is running out.

the citizen is not getting any information about what is really going on in the euro negotiations. i also think that the negotiations and the trialogue, as mrs sargentini has already said, are often only a matter of tweets. but other than that, these are black boxes. the ep refuses to participate in backroom politics and makes the documents public. negotiations can be held in public, or at least a state of play should be announced after each round. all these issues are also mentioned in the sargentini report and i hope that it will be adopted by consensus.
i would like to thank the honourable members for the good proposals which are before us and which i wholeheartedly support. the will to improve is always good, of course. we want a competitive europe with better legislation and less administrative burden. as a politician, i am in favour of more transparency in the creation of regulations, but as a lawyer and european citizen, i think that the practical effect is perhaps even more important. so i have three things to improve on. first, the methodology. for example, the way in which amendments are made: they are often based on national sentiments, but they end up making the legislation complex and illegible. secondly, we still base 80% of our european legislation on directives, which eventually lead to a patchwork of national transposition because they are transposed too late or differently in different countries). i am in favour of, with many experts, basing more legislation on regulations that work directly and ensure a better level playing field. finally, i would like to advocate that we communicate our legislation better to our citizens, that they know why we are working here and that they are also interested in it.

the large debts of many member states are at the heart of this crisis. italy has a debt of 130% of gdp and ireland's budget deficit is 10%. greece has the worst record: the debt ratio is 157% of gdp and the budget deficit is almost 8%. the burden of the greek hero atlas was lighter. greece can never pay off that debt, and so that debt must be written off in part. the participation of private parties is also well regulated. the greek economy also needs a recovery plan to pay off the remaining debt, and the greeks must make a full effort to collect taxes. if these measures are not taken very soon, greece will have to leave the euro. the euro is a quality currency, and this requires discipline and determination, such as being able to deal effectively with debt and prevent bankruptcy.
how do we, mr president, win the confidence of the financial markets in weak euro countries? by cutting back and reforming. both are painful but indispensable for sound finances, improved competitiveness and sufficient earning power. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial markets are not distorted and that the political process is guided by a correct and consistent policy. but a real solution requires more. a number of euro countries have high external debt. this is linked to the imbalances in the trade and balance of payments. a dutch professor advocates a new bretton woods agreement that should balance this, for example by regulating the financial sector from financing high trade deficits of other countries. does the commission and the council see this as a good proposal to reduce this structural imbalance? a second short question: what is the council's view of the dutch proposal to strengthen budgetary discipline?

the necessary infrastructure: water pumps, schools, solar panels, health clinics, roads and the like. the eu could do much better securing the link between emergency action and long-term development efforts. that brings me to the third point. these long-term efforts also need to come from national authorities in the horn of africa. for instance, the government of ethiopia is reluctant to help the pastoralists who lead their nomadic life with their herds. pastoralists are seen as old-fashioned and should, in the official view, move to cities or sedentarise, but sedentarisation is no option in arid and semi-arid areas. these people, these pastoralists, bring an essential part of food, protein, to the population and secure an important part of economic life. they are the only people who can make a living in these arid areas too poor in vegetation to allow herds to stay in one spot. the eu should, in its dialogue with governments, stress the need to recognise the legitimate demands of on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i would like to start by thanking the commissioners and the minister for their constructive contributions to this debate. i will not go back twenty years like karl-heinz florenz, but rather i want to take you back 400 years when william shakespeare wrote his famous words ‘to be or not to be’. i believe that a contemporary and more economically-oriented shakespeare could now write ‘to be sustainable or not to be at all’. because that is the question. the world is currently developing towards a green economy; an economy where sustainability is a key condition for being competitive. leading companies are aware of this and are investing in that vision, but unfortunately most companies still consider sustainability as something which costs a lot of money. for that reason, rio+20 comes at the right moment. it is a huge opportunity to show the world that being efficient with resources, emitting less and investing in green technology does pay off. let rio+20 be a worldwide wake-up call and not only involve the business community but let them commit themselves to the objectives of the green economy. but that is the ‘green economy’ side of the summit; what about the other top issue: a constitutional framework, governance, or politicians?
