in writing. − the sp has fundamental objections to risky innovative eu funding instruments.
the commissioner has just said something that gives hope, because if we do nothing, antibiotics are not the solution, they are the problem for life. every year, 25,000 people in the eu die untreatably because bacteria in their bodies are resistant to antimicrobials. we're using the wrong medicine at the wrong scale and for the wrong purposes. antibiotics should be prescribed by a medical professional for a specific problem. but it's not going the way it's supposed to. half the population is already taking antibiotics for a cold or flu, wrongly. not only are antibiotics prescribed to sick people by a doctor, but healthy people also get them through their food, by eating meat that has been deliberately contaminated with them because antibiotics have been given to animal feed as a preventive measure. the perverse effect is that our livestock farmers in particular are exposed to great health risks from the aggressive mrsa bacteria. we have been blind to the side effects and the long-term effects, including on the environment, for too long. we've always focused on the operation in the present and the effect on the individual, but at the same time we've been saddling the collective and our posterity with deadly consequences. i often have fundamental criticisms of the eu's interventionist approach, but here we have a health problem that really justifies international intervention. crossborder action is the only recipe for curbing this serious health problem. i hope that the council and the commission will not be immune to the action points we have drawn up as parliament, and i hope that the council and the commission will complete the whole course.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. sometimes you can rely on your own experience, and i'll do that here. i know from experience of visiting doctors in, say, belgium or france, that it seems that those doctors do not want to disappoint you as a patient and you come back from the doctor with a big bag full of medication, including very often antibiotics. not to mention a continent like africa, where i was once advised to peel from a large candy jar to take a firm handful of antibiotic tablets! how different is that in my own country, the netherlands. we're very careful about antibiotics in humans. you'd have to beg a doctor on your knees for a cure for your sickness. on the other hand, in the netherlands, farm animals do not have to beg for medicines on their knees, no, they are forced to do so. they're being given large doses of antibiotics without being asked. unfortunately, the netherlands is a champion in the use of antibiotics in animals. mrs mcavan also mentioned the strong lobby of those who doubt the link between use in livestock farming and resistance affecting humans. in the netherlands, pig farmers and their families and people working on pig farms are treated separately in hospitals. just because of the danger of resistant bacteria. that's how big the risk is. the risks are so great that we cannot let this go unchecked. i am therefore very pleased that mr dalli, on behalf of the commission, is planning to come up with a very ambitious proposal. i hope that this will be followed up after 18 november with strong legislation, because unfortunately we cannot leave this to the member states alone.
mr president, this is the second time in six months that we have spoken about antibiotic resistance. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has already taken a number of decisions on the matter. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he has not already made a statement on the matter. but you could also read in it that we may be far too fragmented on this subject. and i think that's where you're really putting your finger on the problem. we're looking at this problem in a way that's far too fragmented. it becomes an animal husbandry versus public health debate, when the two are intertwined and only an integral approach offers a solution. honor where it's due. this resolution does indeed address all these aspects. i think it could have been a little brighter in certain respects. i'll check the ranch first. this is rightly pointed out, because despite some progress, the use in animal husbandry is still far too high. in my member state, the netherlands, all the parties involved, from farmers to supermarkets, have therefore sat down and agreed to reduce consumption by 20% this year and by 50% in 2013. that is a high ambition, commissioner, and perhaps an inspiration for the plans you are going to propose. then the humane consumption. i think the text is still too soft. we have to monitor, we have to inform, that's all fine. but it's just illegal to sell antibiotics without a prescription. research shows that in some member states this prohibition is still circumvented by pharmacies who offer these products to consumers on a mass basis, including without a prescription. we are rightly asking for a lot of effort from the livestock industry, but we are not going to solve the problem of antibiotic resistance as long as in some countries in europe we are still running to the pharmacy for the first cold, and i agree with my colleague from the communist group, running directly to the pharmacy for our next shot. i am therefore pleased, commissioner, we have often been at each other's throats in this chamber, but i am pleased with your proposal and, moreover, i am happy to add a tooth.
we all agree that antibiotic resistance is a life-threatening threat: there are pneumonia outbreaks that can't be treated, hospitals that are dealing with mrsa cases, outbreaks of ehec. we agree on that. you say the approach is difficult, and that's why i have six rules for you against resistance. firstly, we must no longer prescribe preventive medicines but curative ones, i.e. we must treat the animal with antibiotics only when it is sick and then only this animal and not the whole herd. treat a sick animal like a sick human being. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the disease. secondly: the commission has already proposed a number of amendments to the directive. obligation to take the intermediate step via the pharmacy if it is also clear that it can only be supplied on prescription. this was found to lead to a direct and large reduction in use in denmark. moreover, it leads to more surveillance, which brings me to my third point: good statistics on the use and prescription of antibiotics. for what disease, what animal and in what region? this allows comparisons, including with human diseases, and then there is more insight into the possible jump from animal diseases to humans and the emergence of resistance. fourthly: enter a head-up system. reduction to healthy and safe minimum standards. in this way we can compare what is a safe minimum standard and each time there has to be an adjustment from all countries to the safe and healthy minimum standard. fifth: fully invested in research. it has been found that only five of the world's 15 major pharmaceutical companies are involved in antibiotic research. the innovation union has said that we need to avoid fragmentation and duplication. let's say we really put our minds to it and investigate possible alternatives. and sixth: we must end intensive livestock farming, change the consumption of meat, because that's where it all starts. that's the beginning and the end of the whole problem.
tomorrow, mr president, we are voting on a very important directive to combat the sexual abuse and sexual exploitation of children and child pornography. these violations must stop and that is why i support the report. i would like to draw your attention to three points. first of all: cooperation. criminal gangs, active in child pornography and sexual exploitation, often operate across borders. by working together, police and prosecutors can map the entire chain of their malign activities. their punishment must cover all aspects of their work. of course, international criminals will always pretend to operate in relatively small units, in order to minimize their role. but this fable must be exposed. the criminals are traffickers, and they operate ruthlessly and without borders. secondly: victim support. last week, i organized a meeting with my romanian colleague buşoi on human trafficking and sexual exploitation. aid organizations from western and eastern europe were also present. they help women and children who have been victims of sexual exploitation. they do this because various governments do little in this area. in countries such as hungary and romania, women and children who are victims do not always receive the right help. but article 18 of the draft directive says that member states must help child victims. several of the remainder-of-me morgues in central and eastern europe are full of young girls, some as young as 16. i call on the eastern european member states to take the directive to heart and to take victim support seriously, and on the commission to ensure that it is given. thirdly: the prevention of accidents. we find little of that in the directive, and there is much to be improved. i support the rapporteur who calls on national authorities to do more to prevent this. and my question to the commission is: how will member states be accountable for prevention programmes?

rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to start by saying how satisfied i am with the cooperation between the commission, the council and the parliament. the parliament last year set out very clear criteria for the transfer of pnr data to third countries. the commission then put forward a very good package in line with those criteria, and the council very swiftly adopted a negotiating mandate. i think this example demonstrates that, if the three institutions work together, europe is a force to be reckoned with. i would also like to thank the shadow rapporteurs, axel voss, birgit sippel, timothy kirkhope, jan albrecht, rui tavares and, more recently, cornelia ernst. even if we do not agree on every single detail i think the pnr team has worked in a very constructive spirit. as rapporteur, i recommend in my draft report that this house gives its consent to the conclusion of an agreement with australia on the transfer of pnr data. i think the recommendation will get wide support, and i am very happy with that. however, that does not mean that all concerns and reservations have been met. there are still some doubts and hesitations. for this reason, some groups in this house will withhold their vote or vote against. i fully understand their objections, and i share most of their concerns. however, they know as well as i do that a ‘no’ vote will not stop the transfer of data. we are not voting on the actual transfer, we can only vote on the conditions governing the transfer of data. a ‘no’ vote, therefore, is purely symbolic. for that reason, i will also not endorse the gue group’s call for a consultation of the european court of justice, although i have great sympathy for that proposal. we have assessed the draft agreement against the criteria that we set out last year and given a positive assessment on a number of points, in particular the limitation of the ‘purpose’ definition. it has been considerably tightened, and includes only the fight against terrorism and serious transnational crime. i think that is a very important achievement. we have also ensured that the method of transfer will be ‘push’ exclusively. that is very important, also in view of future agreements. there are also some other positive points. however there are also a couple of remaining concerns, as i said initially. the most fundamental point is that the necessity and proportionality of the mass collection and storage of data has been demonstrated only partially, and not sufficiently. secondly, no justification has been given for the long-term storage of identifiable data of all travellers. the retention period of five and a half years appears to be fairly random, and was not based on any specific evidence. finally, the appropriate legal basis for the agreement should be – or in any case include – article 16 of the treaties, on data protection. if you retrace the history of these agreements back to 2003, the purpose of this agreement has been, from the very start, exclusively to ensure that the transfer of data to third countries is in line with eu data protection standards. therefore, the only proper legal base can be article 16 and nothing else. if the purpose were exclusively police and justice cooperation, the eu could theoretically decide against the collection of pnr data by australia. but it cannot, because it is a sovereign and unilateral decision by australia to collect these data. finally, we need a political assurance by the commission that the agreement remains the joint responsibility of the three eu institutions, each with its own role. if, in future, the european parliament notes serious deficiencies in the application of the agreement, we expect the commission to respond and to take appropriate action including, if necessary, the submission of proposals for the suspension or termination of the agreement. i would like to ask the presidency of this house to see to it that these assurances, as expressed today and noted in the minutes, are confirmed by an exchange of letters with the commission.
mr president, it is now clear that the greens will vote against this agreement with australia. it remains completely unclear what australia actually wants to do with the personal data of european travellers. this parliament has asked for a guarantee that australia will not profile the personal data in order to treat passengers who are not suspected differently on the basis of that profile. we didn't get that guarantee. of course, we didn't get that guarantee, because profiling is the reason why governments -- and not just australia, but also the united states and canada and perhaps in the future the european union -- want to collect passenger data. - why? - why? there is still no evidence that storing passenger data helps in the fight against terrorism. if law enforcement agencies are looking for the details of a particular passenger, they can pull them from airlines. they don't throw that information away after the passenger gets out. so you don't have to keep it somewhere in the government for five and a half years. five and a half years in australia, and decades in the united states, and who knows how long the retention period in the european pnr will be for the future. i reject the rapporteur's argument that this agreement with australia has a positive effect on the negotiations with the usa and canada. this agreement seems to me to be more of a harbinger of how bad the next agreements can be. long retention periods, the transfer of data to other agencies, even foreign intelligence services, and the risk of europeans being denied entry on the basis of their profile. no, this is not a symbolic 'no' vote by the greens, because if this parliament were to say 'no', that agreement would be illegal and the transfer of data would be illegal. that is why we support the request for an inquiry into the fundamental rights in the pnr.
rapporteur. − madam president, first of all i would like to address a few words to my friends in the green group and the gue/ngl group, though unfortunately jan albrecht has left. we are politicians. we are not activists. sometimes we face very different dilemmas. i think everybody in this house – and this has been expressed by every single speaker – is facing those dilemmas, but we have to take tough decisions. sometimes it hurts, and it hurts all of us – even the ones who are going to vote in favour. i would like to say to ms sargentini, who spoke about the consequences of rejecting the agreement, saying that, if we reject the agreement, the transfer of data will become illegal and can no longer be transferred. that is true, but then what? are we going to suspend air travel altogether? i recognise that this would fit in with another green objective, namely that of reducing co2 emissions, which is a good objective, but it is not very realistic. frankly, unless you can provide a credible alternative, a ‘no’ vote is purely symbolic and cheap. secondly, i would like to remind colleagues that we have to stick together because this is only the first step, as my colleague mr kelly rightly said. this is the first agreement. it is also the first step in a long process. it is the first reversal of the trend of unrestricted collection of data, and we are facing other negotiations. we are in the middle of very tough negotiations with canada and the united states. it is more important than ever that we stick together, that we show the rest of the world that europe is united and that we do stand up for fundamental rights. in this respect i would very much like to echo in particular the words of my colleague, ms sippel, about ‘push-pull’. we have emphasised very strongly how important it is that this agreement concerns ‘push’ only and not ‘pull’. that was in parliament’s resolution and we will stick by it in the agreement with australia and in other agreements that follow.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). a credible and effective approach is therefore urgently needed. as far as is known, the main lines of the agreement reached have been received with a moderate degree of approval. however, there are important caveats. first, market confidence will have to be restored on the basis of the quality of governance in the deeply indebted member states. above all, investors must now determine whether this package is compelling. secondly: the size of the emergency fund. it is not as high as economists and central bankers would like. the question is whether this element is future-proof. thirdly: the privatisation programme of greece. it is unclear on what the optimism for an additional 15 billion in privatisations is based. the results achieved so far do not give sufficient reason for such optimism. fourthly: the level of elaboration. i am thinking for example of the value of the private sector write-downs on greek government bonds, a crucial element on which the banking sector needs to work with the authorities so that this is clear by the end of 2011. the commission has also taken a number of measures to ensure that the banking sector is not left behind. the european banking authority has yet to work out its business on the basis of more recent september capital requirements figures, and estimates have shown considerable variation in this area in recent times. a fifth crucial point concerns economic governance. the question is: are there sufficient safeguards to prevent future problems? on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, representatives of the council and the commission, ladies and gentlemen, the epp group has enshrined and supported net neutrality as one of the main principles in the 2009 telecoms package. we want to use it to spur innovation, to expand the number of internet services. it's a miracle that even in this time of crisis, we can do that. the number of jobs is increasing rapidly, and this is a real growth sector, perhaps the largest in europe. broadband opens up a whole new world, a world of functions for citizens, for businesses and for governments. function and function go together here very strongly. the commission is now considering the possibility of implementing this directive in the member states. there are network operators who actually block new providers, presenting unnecessarily high bills. this was also the case in the netherlands. herbert reul has already given other examples. that's how skype and whatsapp were blocked. the netherlands is therefore considering a supplementary bill. there is nothing wrong with that in itself, but it cannot be that all countries will set up their own regulations, which will eventually lead to the surrender of the one internet space in europe. the added value of the one digital space, an open internet, is beyond question for us. finally, both committees are now sending a signal to the member states to be clear about transposition. secondly, for us, for the epp, new regulation need not be the answer, provided that we do give targeted implementation. in principle, we support net neutrality, we have high expectations of functions, of innovation and of jobs. that's what we're about.
mr president, the internet was created with no specific use in mind other than the efficient transfer of information. over the last 20 years the internet and technology have developed at an extremely rapid pace, giving rise to huge economic and social benefits. the key driver of this unprecedented innovation has been that all information flows and services are treated more or less equally, conforming with the principle of net neutrality. recently, a major telecoms provider in the netherlands bragged to shareholders about its throttling of the voice over ip and messaging services that directly compete with its core business of selling text messages and calling minutes. within hours it became known that all major providers in the netherlands did the same. the dutch parliament was quick to react and legally ensured net neutrality. it is not the job of governments to protect certain business models, and the dutch minister said that the company had clearly overplayed its hand. consumers pay too much, and transparency clearly has not worked in practice. i do not imagine that dutch telecom providers are the only ones guilty of these practices, and we need to ensure that there is an eu-wide level playing field. the research by berec will show what is happening, and perhaps the dutch example can serve as an eu-wide example. in any case, the wait-and-see approach of the commission with regard to net neutrality is hurting consumers, innovation, competition and society as a whole. transparency from providers and the ability for consumers to switch easily between providers are not enough on their own, and deep-packet traffic inspection methods are threatening fundamental rights.
president, commissioner, are your colleagues mr de gucht and mr kroes saying that no measures are to be expected for the time being? but that is what the european parliament wants. the netherlands had to adopt a kind of emergency law in june 2011, which legally mandates net neutrality, as the first country in the european union, which was a direct consequence of the fact that our largest telecom operator kpn wanted to make customers pay for competing online services such as skype or whatsapp, and that while the first telecom package of 2009 had already established that it must be net neutral throughout europe. it is obviously a bit crazy to say that 'neutral' really means neutral, and that it is not for the company to interpret neutrality, but for us, the legislator, and that we must guarantee it, and that it cannot be that, because of changing consumer habits, services are blocked or taxed. i really want to argue for the avoidance of 27 different interpretations of neutrality. we must act now, we do not want to have some kind of emergency laws, because a free and neutral internet is the economic, communication backbone of our society and must be transparent, qualitative and neutral.

the european parliament has repeatedly called for an effective and joint european policy to promote the integration of roma into the eu, because the practice in some member states leaves much to be desired. in april the european commission opted for european guidelines, for national strategies. in december, the member states will come up with a plan, if it is good. i ask the commission: how far are these plans and are you confident that they will succeed, and how will you ensure this? because all too often we hear alarming reports, for example of attacks on roma in bulgaria at a bus stop with boxing bracelets and slogans such as 'dirty gypsies'. in some member states, far-right political parties and politicians have intensified anti-roma rhetoric and actions. discrimination and violent attacks against roma are still too common. forced evictions also remain a major problem. it is sad that nowhere in the eu is the situation of europe's largest minority in a rosy light. the need for strategies was very much needed. but objectives and intentions are not enough. some member states, for example, do not want a target group policy, as the netherlands does. how does the commission, with 27 different strategies, guarantee that good and effective solutions to the roma problem will now be found? how does the commissioner intend to coordinate this? roma are eu citizens with rights like any other.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i can fully agree with the previous two speakers with whom we have worked on this joint motion for a resolution. the convention on cluster munitions entered into force in 2010 and most member states were included. it is important to ban the use of cluster munitions because they cause excessive and indiscriminate damage to civilians. from a humanitarian point of view, cluster munitions are a disaster. regrettably some member states believe that cluster munitions are important for their security and their economy. i will not dispute the importance of security, but there are more sophisticated and less damaging ways to ensure it. apart from use, there is trade. most recently, we heard reports of the use of cluster munitions in libya and apparently some member states were involved in providing them. there will shortly be a discussion on protocol vi during the review conference of certain conventional weapons. this protocol is not compatible with commitments made by member states. on the contrary, instead of undermining the convention on cluster munitions, we want more member states to subscribe. they would join member states who will not use, develop, produce, stockpile or transfer cluster munitions or engage in any of these prohibited activities. the eu should press member states and candidate member states, and be a force for a global ban on cluster munitions. if we want to be a credible actor in pushing for this important goal, we must lead by example with the inclusion of all member states.
mr president, shipping is a vital part of european transport and the european economy. a lot of jobs and innovation have come from the maritime cluster. and rightly so, europe is now investing in strengthening this sector. i always consider that a win-win situation. the commission has now allocated 40 million to the integrated maritime policy incentive fund. i would like to see, mr president, that part of that money also be spent on training young people who are interested in seafaring, so that they too become aware of the opportunities of seafaring and the relationship between seafaring and ecology. i hope that's possible. i would also like to see green shipping technology supported by this fund. in any case, no money to consultants or all sorts of boards or all sorts of people who are going to discuss strategies. never much comes of it. let's not do that. finally, i would like to thank my colleague, mr georgios koumoutsakos, very much. we worked well together and from a complex subject you managed to put together a good report. thank you very much.

president. - the eurozone is under pressure. let's be clear about that. drastic intervention by the european central bank and the introduction of euro bonds can only temporarily relieve the pressure on the financial system. europe must use the time to address the underlying problems. first, a controlled reduction in unsustainably high levels of debt in the public sector, the private sector and, in particular, the banks. secondly, to reduce the destabilising effects of large differences in economic competitiveness between member states on the financial system. the question that now bursts forth with ever greater force: can the measures agreed or being discussed in the run-up to the european council on 8 and 9 december provide a satisfactory answer? can stricter budgetary discipline and assistance from the international monetary fund suffice? i hope so, in view of short-term financial stability, but i am seriously concerned about the longer term. the question is whether a move towards a eurozone with stricter conditions, which it is feared that some member states cannot meet at present, can be avoided. it takes time to work out the necessary solutions. perhaps we should jointly reorganise the euro area temporarily and thoroughly in order to achieve greater stability in the financial system and the economy in the medium term. this offers a perspective for all the countries of the euro zone and the european union.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. - president, the eu-20-20 goals and the european semester could in theory help us to make a great step forward. the member states have set themselves specific objectives: 75% participation of men and women, lifting 20 million people out of poverty, fighting climate change, investing in innovation, and they also have their measures to achieve these targets monitored by the commission. totally great, so far. but in practice, it doesn't work, or not well enough. member states have far too low ambitions to achieve the 2020 targets together. some reform programmes are really of ridiculously low quality and social objectives are systematically forgotten. and austerity sounds great, even if it means that 20 million more people are falling into poverty. the commission's comments are, to say the least, incoherent. why isn't it working now? actually, there's only one simple answer: the european semester has no audience. at eu level, the european parliament has too little say, at national level, parliaments, social partners and ngos are not involved. nobody knows about it, nobody has any control over it, and so nobody cares. no minister is going to be called on by the public or the press if his work is severely understated. the chances of a government being blamed for failing in the european semester at the next election are nil. if we're going to make this project work, we need to get the audience. national parliaments, social partners and ngos who hold their governments to their own promises. a european parliament that can speak with all its might. the excellent report by mrs berès lists all kinds of concrete measures to ensure this. we are very happy to vote in favour of this report and look forward to its adoption as soon as possible. we are already starting to do so in the committee on employment, and i have the honour of being rapporteur for a report by the european parliament on the priorities for the coming year. i would ask the commission and the council to listen very carefully to this report.

president, commissioner, the neighbourhood policy has our warm support, especially with the new course that has now been chosen. i'm just going to take one little part out. that is the enpi, the instrument for cooperation across external borders. these are small-scale projects, sometimes from hospitals together, sometimes from universities together, tangible projects from person to person. in 2007-2013 we transferred money from the regional fund to this end . we have looked at how that works now in a parliamentary study, a hearing. we have seen that this has been very difficult. we also saw that the emphasis was particularly on the capital cities and that people in the region, partners, cities, had a very difficult time getting ahead. the report makes clear in paragraph 8 that in future more of these erdf methods, more interreg cooperation methods, should be used. i think that's a very important signal. we just have to take that to improve efficiency and also flexibility. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the polish presidency has now also described this in its conclusions. so support this approach, but the enpi must work better and more cross-border. the focus should not be on policies in the capitals, but on projects close to the people. commissioner, how are we gonna do this? i am a member of the regi committee and i would like to work with you on this in the next regulation. we had an enhanced procedure now. i think we're going to get mr. brok and his people back.
mr president, how far removed this house is from reality and reality. i think this legislation, this so-called compromise on fruit juice, is an example of that. i'll tell you what the reality is. the average european runs into the supermarket five minutes before closing time with two tired children after work and is confronted with a whole wall of fruit juices and fruit drinks, including fruit nectar, as it is called. i asked a number of people in the supermarket what they thought of when they heard the word "nectar". and they think about bees and natural products. they think of everything and more. but the last thing they think about is that sugar has been added to this so-called natural product. that's the case. the fine print is not going to be read by the average european, because he has two crying children in his cart. he doesn't have time for that. i therefore considered the commission's original proposal to be more than justified. the proposal proposed that the packaging should clearly and explicitly state that sugar has been added to these fruit drinks, these nectars. but there is nothing left of that proposal. we no longer explicitly label added sugars, whereas we note that fruit juices, which do not contain sugar, do not contain sugar. that's the world upside down. we label what's not in it, and what's in it we leave out. i am surprised that the commission is defending this now, because when its proposal came out there was a nice press release saying that we are going to inform the consumer better about added sugar. now, that's far from the case. first a lot of bombshell, and now they're agreeing to this half-baked compromise. i am also surprised that colleagues from the left-wing groups, who are always so keen to inform consumers, are actually causing confusion amongst consumers. unfortunately, i will be in the minority, but i will vote against this compromise tomorrow.

in writing. − i would like to underline forcefully that, by voting in favour of this report, i by no means accept the reference to the definition of ‘terrorism’ as it is formulated in framework decision 2002/475/jha on combating terrorism. i wish to state clearly that i reject such a broad definition, which could include virtually every act that has some element of organisation or planning, and which raises the legitimate question of whether this instrument has built-in ‘function creep’ in order not only to combat terrorism but also to extend the definition in such a manner as to cover most public order situations and more common criminal acts, as they apply under national law.
in writing. − i would like to underline forcefully that by voting in favour of this report, i by no means accept the reference to the definition of ‘terrorism’ as it is formulated in the framework decision 2002/475/jha on combating terrorism. i wish to state clearly that i reject such a broad definition which could virtually include every act that has some element of organisation or planning and that legitimately raises the question whether this instruments has a built-in ‘function creep’ in order not only to combat terrorism but to extend the definition in such a manner as to cover most public order situations and more common criminal acts as they apply under national law. like other legislation of this nature (in the us for instance), we must be very careful to not leave any room for draconian measures to enter from the backdoor to control political dissent, demonstrations, protests, and must be very vigilant that it will not be used to curb the freedom of expression.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0177/97). a democracy requires openness. democracy requires that people can check whether the decision that has been made for them is the right decision. we no longer have direct democracy, we have people's representatives and we have ministers in, for example, the european council. but it is still of the utmost importance that what we do is controlled by another. and it seems that the christian democrats in this house want a kind of old-fashioned form of government, namely, 'go to sleep, we'll take care of you and everything will be fine.' but it is precisely in a europe of 27 member states, or perhaps 26 member states plus one, that it is very crucial that we can weigh up which country or which group represents which interest. mistakes are easier to make behind a closed door than behind an open one. so we will have to give europeans access to decision-making. a closed door also leads to more euroscepticism. how can you be sure that your minister did the right thing for you? he can tell you that at home, but you can't check. and that is what this european parliament should be about. i think it is naive for the commission and the council, and also the christian democrats, to say: 'no, that is just a nuisance and we do not want to bother the europeans with that, it will only take time and we would need the lobbyists at the table'. the lobbyists are at the table, and i would like to know at which table which lobbyists are at which table and who is being influenced by them. that is why the european parliament is asking for transparency and the europeans are asking for it.
i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals. for us meps, brussels may be the centre of our work, but for the average citizen, brussels is very far away. moreover, recent research shows that citizens across europe are losing confidence in european policies. more than half of the citizens see an increase in poverty in europe as a result of brussels. the traditional response is to explain it to the citizen again. the information budget of the european institutions is enormous, but all this broadcasting is not working. the citizen of today is no longer a passive receiver. he wants to be heard, and he wants his voice to matter. he'll decide what he wants to know. the rapporteur has chosen in his proposal a strong approach to the content, which gives the citizen more - and not less - right to information, as the commission would like. i am grateful to him for having accepted my suggestions that documents relating to negotiations in the council should not be made inaccessible to the public in advance. it is precisely at the negotiating stage that the citizen wishes to make his voice heard. and we can't take that away from him. it will be difficult negotiations with the commission and the council, for which the rapporteur needs the support of the whole parliament. i hope that the christian democrats in particular will come to their senses in time and vote for the citizen, instead of indulging in backroom politics.

on behalf of the ecr group. - mr president, first of all i would like to thank mr fleckenstein for his work and also for the pleasant cooperation and also thank emsa. i think emsa is one of the better european agencies. emsa can detect oil spills with satellite systems and emsa satellite data can also be used in the fight against piracy. so all points that are good for safety at sea. however, mr president, i think that europe is going to make a big deal of this revision of the emsa mandate. the proposals give emsa far more tasks and powers than are necessary. for example, look at the tasks that emsa is given to promote the internal market at sea or to deal with inland navigation. these are the kinds of things that i say should be left out of emsa. this is already being done in a good way by a number of national and international organisations. there is no need for emsa to duplicate or to switch. the same applies to the inspection of oil and gas platforms. i think it is unnecessary for emsa to do that. the monitoring of these platforms is already taking place in the european countries and i myself know that monitoring very well. i have worked closely with the inspection service and i know that the monitoring of the gas and oil platforms is generally well organized. the fleckenstein report also opens the door to a european coastguard. mr president, my group does not want that. firstly, not because that work is already being done in an excellent way by the national coastguards, but also - which many people forget - because the national coastguards are already working very well together. they are also effective, so that further step does not need to be taken as far as my group is concerned. in short, mr president, we must once again say that enough is enough. in this parliament we are always asking for more tasks and more powers. that's a step too far for us now! despite the good work of the rapporteur, we cannot support his report.

- a writer. - mr president, the fact that there are prisons in europe where prisoners, suspects and convicts are exposed to the risk of disease and violence is a disgrace in itself. it is unheard of that some member states, like the commission, still speak of mutual trust in the rule of law; it is only a matter of time before one of the courts, luxembourg or strasbourg, prohibits a member state from expelling a resident on the basis of article 3 of the european convention on human rights, the article dealing with torture and inhuman treatment. the dutchman robert hörchner spent eight months in a polish prison because of a european arrest warrant. he came out skinny - not to say emaciated - and with scabies all over his body. he was acquitted, by the way. this parliament does not want this kind of thing! so this parliament is not only calling for better, cleaner, safer prisons, but certainly for a review of the principle of mutual trust without control. how is it that in times of debt crisis, member states which distrust each other in the field of finance ask for sanctions, for checks and balances, for controls, but when it comes to the expulsion of a resident from one country to another, mutual trust is considered good enough? this parliament is therefore calling not only for safe prisons but also for a maximum period of detention and for the possibility for a judge to refuse an expulsion order on the basis of the european arrest warrant and not to extradite a resident to another country where the detention facilities are such that article 3 is violated. because, mr president, trust is good, but control is better.
mr president, for better european cooperation in the area of justice it is crucial that the member states can trust in each other’s justice systems. a shortcoming in detention conditions is one reason for this lack of trust. even though detention conditions in some member states might be worse than others, none of the member states have a clear conscience when it comes to detention conditions. problems such as overcrowded prisons and ill-treatment of the detainees are to be observed throughout the eu. what is even more alarming are the cases of death and suicide in detention. in the resolution we asked the member states to prevent suicides in prison and to carry out in-depth and impartial investigations, systematically in cases where a prisoner dies in prison. six months after the death of i̇hsan gürz in police detention in the netherlands, there are still several versions of what happened leading up to his death. this makes it difficult for i̇hsan’s family to have closure. unfortunately there are similar cases throughout europe. therefore we are urging independent investigations to be conducted when someone dies in detention. the detention conditions are perhaps the responsibility of member states but their shortcomings damage the trust which is needed to achieve judicial cooperation in criminal matters at european level. the lack of trust is preventing member states from extraditing criminals. what does the commission intend to do to ensure transparency in every member state as to what happens in detention and how is the eu intending to ensure the fundamental rights of the detainees?
president. - mr president, five years ago bulgaria and romania became members of our union. the accession treaties stipulate that all other member states will open their labour markets to romanian and bulgarian workers either immediately or gradually. the final phase of transition is now under way, a phase in which only those countries which can prove a serious disturbance of their labour markets may take transitional measures. there is now also a comprehensive report - for which we are grateful to you - which shows that countries which have not introduced transitional measures have actually benefited from this, and that transitional measures have mainly negative effects. all the member states therefore have every reason to open their labour markets fully to bulgarian and romanian workers. unfortunately, a number of countries have announced that they are still willing to take restrictive measures, with the netherlands being the most enthusiastic about worst practice. if it is up to minister kamp of social affairs, no bulgarian and no romanian will be allowed to enter the netherlands next year. facts have nothing to do with this. not only is it absolutely ridiculous that in the netherlands, one of the richest countries in the eu with the lowest unemployment, it is claimed that there could be a disruption of the labour market, but there is also all kinds of research - also in the netherlands - that shows that the measures promote exploitation. transitional measures mean condemning thousands of eu citizens to exploitation, poor working conditions, false self-employment and underpayment. transitional measures also mean that thousands of employers cannot recruit the workers they think are best suited to work in their company. transitional measures are, in my view, a violation of the right of free movement and a violation of the accession treaties if a country cannot prove serious infringement. you, commissioner, are the guardian of the treaties. i expect you to carry them out not only in letter but in spirit. so as soon as you receive the notification from the netherlands, i expect you to put aside the investigation and the facts and say, mr. kamp, hurry up with your ridiculous story. if you do not, the accession treaties are not worth the paper they are printed on. the netherlands has always been there like a chicken to remind romania and bulgaria of all their obligations, and now they are running away with their own obligations under these treaties which they themselves have ratified. if you let the netherlands get away with this, you are basically saying that domestic political relations are more important than the free movement of workers. you are saying that the richer old member states can interpret the treaties as they see fit, even if it is to the detriment of their own employers and thousands of romanians and bulgarians.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the basis of the proposals contained in the white paper on growth and employment. i will be discussing the future of the fund after 2014. we have all been eagerly awaiting the commission's proposals to improve the fund. there is a lot to be done to make it the real rapid intervention fund we had in mind, and something must also be done about the fact that it is mainly the richer member states that use it, not the poorer ones. what we didn't see coming at all were the farmers. i was really amazed to read the proposal. what strikes me most is that you want to make two totally different instruments out of it. a small social instrument for laid-off workers and a larger instrument of 2.5 billion for farmers. i think you have to choose: they're the same, or they're different. if they are the same, then farmers can be covered by the egf under the same rules. i'd like that. or they can be treated equally by bringing the crisis- and globalisation-hit companies under the same rules you now propose for farmers. if eu policies or a free trade agreement threaten to hit them, they can restructure their company before it goes bankrupt. the flemish textile industry and the spanish ceramic industry had benefited enormously from the free trade with asia, which had forced their products out of the market. but in that case you have a very different instrument, and it becomes an industrial instrument, and then you can add a few billion. i think the commission really wants something quite different with agriculture. so take that decision and come up with a suitable instrument which can be debated in the agricultural community. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
i appreciate the commission's attempt to simplify, to set clear objectives and to introduce what you call mainstreaming, but we do not need to reinvent the wheel. if something just works, it works, and you don't have to come back to it in a different way or call it something else. then it's all right. the intelligent energy for europe programme worked, as you say, it reached the citizen and it reached smes. indeed, as my colleagues have said, it takes so long to get as close as the european union, also on this subject. your colleague oettinger said the same thing yesterday. he said that the programme was already planned and that he would let us know later in the week under what terms. i just think we've been a little unnecessarily agitated. i would like to know in advance what these things are and what they mean. i would like to ask you, mr president, to give me a few words on the subject of the budget. is it expanding, and how will you measure its impact in the future? and, above all, will we have it again, for example, in the next multiannual framework programme and the next horizon programme? can't these kinds of programs just stay ordinary? i would also like to say that i think that the horizon 2020 programme continues to pay great attention to research. that's a good thing. it is precisely these programmes, which are very practical and benefit smes, that are far too few and need more attention.

author. − mr president, yesterday we awarded the sakharov prize to leading human rights defenders from north africa and the middle east, and one of the awardees was mohamed bouazizi, whose self-immolation sparked the uprisings in tunisia, and consequently in many other countries. the award was certainly intended and is intended to support those who continue on the long, difficult path of transition and the struggle towards free and just societies. zakaria bouguira’s case does not necessarily give us cause for hope. he witnessed and filmed the abuse of power and the use of violence by the tunisian police against moroccan soccer supporters, and as a result he was attacked by around 20 police officers. tunisia should do away with these ben ali-style practices and oppression. besides respect for human rights, the rule of law and good governance, we are specifically concerned with the role that technologies can play. they can be used to enable free speech, freedom of the press and access to information and, as this case shows, the documentation of human rights violations. it would be a huge mistake and missed opportunity if surveillance, censorship, tracking and tracing technologies continue to be deployed against people, as happened under the ben ali government.
